E.2
Proof of
Theorem
4.2

For
MDP

5
5

5

Throughout this section, we use a ”tilde” over the variables, e.g. in
\tilde{M}
,
\tilde{\pi}
, etc., to signify that they correspond to the artificially constructed OPE problem in
Algorithm
1
for analyzing trajectory data.

\widetilde{M}
and policies
{{\widetilde{\pi}}_{\mathrm{e}}}
and
{{\widetilde{\pi}}_{b}}
, we have the following properties:

Lemma 20
.

Suppose under
OPE
problem
\textsc{OPE}(\widetilde{M},{{\widetilde{\pi}}_{\mathrm{e}}},{{\widetilde{\pi}}_{\mathrm{e}}},\mathcal{F})
is the output of
Algorithm
1
after inputing
OPE
problem
\textsc{OPE}(M,{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}},{\pi_{b}},\mathcal{F})
and arbitrary integer
K
. Then,

(a)

The standard concentrability of
\widetilde{M}
and
M
satisfies that

\mathsf{C}(\widetilde{M},\widetilde{d}_{h}^{{\widetilde{\pi}}_{b}}(\cdot;\widetilde{M})\leq 2\mathsf{C}(M,d_{h}^{\pi_{b}}(\cdot;M)).

(b)

\widetilde{Q}
calculated in
(
42
)
equal to the state-action value function of
MDP

\widetilde{M}
. Especially, the value functions of
\widetilde{M}
and
M
satisfies

\displaystyle\widetilde{V}(\widetilde{\rho};\widetilde{M})=V(\rho;M),

(43)

where
\widetilde{\rho}
and
\rho
are initial distributions of
\widetilde{M}
and
M
respectively.

Proof of
Lemma
20
.

We first prove
Lemma
20

(
a
)

. We only need to verify that for any
1\leq h\leq H
and
1\leq k\leq K
,

\frac{d_{h}^{{{\widetilde{\pi}}_{\mathrm{e}}}}(({x}_{h},k),{a};\widetilde{M})}{d_{h}^{{{\widetilde{\pi}}_{b}}}(({x}_{h},k),{a};\widetilde{M})}\leq 2\cdot\frac{d_{h}^{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}({x}_{h},{a};M)}{d_{h}^{\pi_{b}}({x}_{h},{a};M)}.

(44)

Our first observation is that
d_{h}^{{{\widetilde{\pi}}_{\mathrm{e}}}}(({x}_{h},k);\widetilde{M})=d^{{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}}_{h}({x}_{h};M).
This can be proved via induction on
(h-1)K+k
. When
(h-1)K+k=1
, i.e.
h=k=1
, we have

d_{h}^{{{\widetilde{\pi}}_{\mathrm{e}}}}(({x}_{h},k);\widetilde{M})=\widetilde{\rho}({x}_{1})=\rho({x}_{1})=d^{{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}}_{h}({x}_{h};M).

Next, we will do the induction. When
k\geq 2
, we have
\widetilde{T}(({x}_{h},k)\mid({x}_{h},k-1),{{\widetilde{\pi}}_{\mathrm{e}}}(({x}_{h},k-1)))=1
, which implies

d_{h}^{{{\widetilde{\pi}}_{\mathrm{e}}}}(({x}_{h},k);\widetilde{M})=d_{h}^{{{\widetilde{\pi}}_{\mathrm{e}}}}(({x}_{h},k-1);\widetilde{M})=d^{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}({x}_{h};M).

When
k=1
, we have
\widetilde{T}(({x}_{h},1)\mid({x}_{h-1},K),{{\widetilde{\pi}}_{\mathrm{e}}}(({x}_{h-1},K)))=T({x}_{h}\mid{x}_{h-1},{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}({x}_{h-1}))
. Hence by induction, we have

\displaystyle d_{h}^{{{\widetilde{\pi}}_{\mathrm{e}}}}(({x}_{h},1);\widetilde{M})

\displaystyle=\sum_{{x}_{h-1}\in\mathcal{X}_{h-1}}d_{h}^{{{\widetilde{\pi}}_{\mathrm{e}}}}(({x}_{h-1},K);\widetilde{M})\widetilde{T}(({x}_{h},1)\mid({x}_{h-1},K),{{\widetilde{\pi}}_{\mathrm{e}}}(({x}_{h-1},K)))

\displaystyle=\sum_{{x}_{h-1}\in\mathcal{X}_{h-1}}d_{h-1}^{{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}}({x}_{h-1};M)T({x}_{h}\mid{x}_{h-1},{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}({x}_{h-1}))

\displaystyle=d^{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}_{h}({x}_{h};M).

Our next observation is that
d_{h}^{{{\widetilde{\pi}}_{b}}}(({x}_{h},k);\widetilde{M})=d^{{\pi_{b}}}_{h}({x}_{h};M)
. This can be proved via induction on
(h-1)K+k
. When
(h-1)K+k=1
, i.e.
h=k=1
, we have

d_{h}^{{{\widetilde{\pi}}_{b}}}(({x}_{h},k);\widetilde{M})=\widetilde{\rho}({x}_{1})=\rho({x}_{1})=d^{{\pi_{b}}}_{h}({x}_{h};M).

Next, we will do the induction. When
k\geq 2
, according to the transition model of
\widetilde{M}
, we have

\widetilde{T}(({x}_{h},k)\mid({x}_{h}^{\prime},k-1),{\pi_{c}}({x}_{h}^{\prime})))=d_{h}^{\pi_{b}}({x}_{h}),

which indicates that

\displaystyle d_{h}^{{{\widetilde{\pi}}_{b}}}(({x}_{h},k);\widetilde{M})

\displaystyle=\sum_{{x}_{h}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}_{h}}d_{h}^{{{\widetilde{\pi}}_{b}}}(({x}_{h}^{\prime},k-1);\widetilde{M})\widetilde{T}(({x}_{h},k)\mid({x}_{h}^{\prime},k-1),{{\widetilde{\pi}}_{b}}(({x}_{h}^{\prime},k-1))))

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{{x}_{h}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}_{h}}d_{h}^{{\pi_{b}}}({x}_{h}^{\prime};M)\widetilde{T}(({x}_{h},k)\mid({x}_{h}^{\prime},k-1),{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}({x}_{h}^{\prime})))

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{{x}_{h}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}_{h}}d_{h}^{{\pi_{b}}}({x}_{h}^{\prime};M)\widetilde{T}(({x}_{h},k)\mid({x}_{h}^{\prime},k-1),{\pi_{c}}({x}_{h}^{\prime})))

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}d_{h}^{{\pi_{b}}}({x}_{h};M)+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{{x}_{h}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}_{h}}d_{h}^{{\pi_{b}}}({x}_{h}^{\prime};M)d_{h}^{\pi_{b}}({x}_{h};M)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}d_{h}^{\pi_{b}}({x}_{h};M)

When
k=1
, we have
\widetilde{T}(({x}_{h},1)\mid({x}_{h-1},K),{{\widetilde{\pi}}_{b}}(({x}_{h-1},K)))=T({x}_{h}\mid{x}_{h-1},{\pi_{b}}({x}_{h-1}))
. Hence by induction, we have

\displaystyle d_{h}^{{{\widetilde{\pi}}_{b}}}(({x}_{h},1);\widetilde{M})

\displaystyle=\sum_{{x}_{h-1}\in\mathcal{X}_{h-1}}d_{h}^{{{\widetilde{\pi}}_{b}}}(({x}_{h-1},K);\widetilde{M})\widetilde{T}(({x}_{h},1)\mid({x}_{h-1},K),{{\widetilde{\pi}}_{b}}(({x}_{h-1},K)))

\displaystyle=\sum_{{x}_{h-1}\in\mathcal{X}_{h-1}}d_{h-1}^{{\pi_{b}}}({x}_{h-1};M)T({x}_{h}\mid{x}_{h-1},{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}({x}_{h-1}))

\displaystyle=d^{\pi_{b}}_{h}({x}_{h};M).

Our final observation is that for
a\neq{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}({x}_{h})
, we always have

d_{h}^{{{\widetilde{\pi}}_{\mathrm{e}}}}(({x}_{h},k),{a};\widetilde{M})=0,

hence
(
44
)
holds. For
a={\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}({x}_{h})
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{d_{h}^{{{\widetilde{\pi}}_{\mathrm{e}}}}(({x}_{h},k),{a};\widetilde{M})}{d_{h}^{{{\widetilde{\pi}}_{b}}}(({x}_{h},k),{a};\widetilde{M})}

\displaystyle=\frac{d_{h}^{{{\widetilde{\pi}}_{\mathrm{e}}}}(({x}_{h},k);\widetilde{M})}{d_{h}^{{{\widetilde{\pi}}_{b}}}(({x}_{h},k);\widetilde{M})}\cdot\frac{{{\widetilde{\pi}}_{\mathrm{e}}}({a}\mid({x}_{h},k))}{{{\widetilde{\pi}}_{b}}({a}\mid({x}_{h},k))},

\displaystyle\frac{d_{h}^{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}({x}_{h},{a};M)}{d_{h}^{\pi_{b}}({x}_{h},{a};M)}

\displaystyle=\frac{d_{h}^{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}({x}_{h};M)}{d_{h}^{\pi_{b}}({x}_{h};M)}\cdot\frac{{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}({a}\mid{x}_{h})}{{\pi_{b}}({a}\mid{x}_{h})}.

According to our construction of
{{\widetilde{\pi}}_{\mathrm{e}}}
and
{{\widetilde{\pi}}_{b}}
, we further have

\frac{{{\widetilde{\pi}}_{\mathrm{e}}}({a}\mid({x}_{h},K))}{{{\widetilde{\pi}}_{b}}({a}\mid({x}_{h},K))}=\frac{{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}({a}\mid{x}_{h})}{{\pi_{b}}({a}\mid{x}_{h})}.

And for
k\neq K
, we have

\frac{{{\widetilde{\pi}}_{\mathrm{e}}}({a}\mid({x}_{h},k))}{{{\widetilde{\pi}}_{b}}({a}\mid({x}_{h},k))}=2.

After noticing that
{\pi_{b}}({\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}({x}_{h})\mid{x}_{h})\leq 1
, we obtain that for
a={\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}({x}_{h})
,

\frac{{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}({a}\mid{x}_{h})}{{\pi_{b}}({a}\mid{x}_{h})}\geq 1=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{{{\widetilde{\pi}}_{\mathrm{e}}}({a}\mid({x}_{h},k))}{{{\widetilde{\pi}}_{b}}({a}\mid({x}_{h},k))}.

Hence as long as
{a}={\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}({x}_{h})
, we always have

\frac{{{\widetilde{\pi}}_{\mathrm{e}}}({a}\mid({x}_{h},K))}{{{\widetilde{\pi}}_{b}}({a}\mid({x}_{h},K))}\leq 2\cdot\frac{{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}({a}\mid{x}_{h})}{{\pi_{b}}({a}\mid{x}_{h})}.

This implies

\displaystyle\frac{d_{h}^{{{\widetilde{\pi}}_{\mathrm{e}}}}(({x}_{h},k),{a};\widetilde{M})}{d_{h}^{{{\widetilde{\pi}}_{b}}}(({x}_{h},k),{a};\widetilde{M})}\leq 2\cdot\frac{d_{h}^{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}({x}_{h},{a};M)}{d_{h}^{\pi_{b}}({x}_{h},{a};M)}.

Next, we will prove
Lemma
20

(
b
)

by induction on
l\vcentcolon={}(h-1)H+k
from
\widetilde{H}
to
1
. When
l=\widetilde{H}
, we have

\widetilde{Q}(({x}_{H},1),{a})=Q({x}_{H},{a})=0,\quad\forall{x}_{H}\in\mathcal{X}_{H},{a}\in\mathcal{A},

which satisfies the induction hypothesis. Next, assuming the induction hypothesis holds for
l+1
, we will prove the induction hypothesis for
l
. We assume
l=(h-1)K+k
.

When
k=K
, according to Bellman equation and induction hypothesis, we have

\displaystyle\widetilde{Q}(({x}_{h},K),{a})

\displaystyle=\widetilde{r}(({x}_{h},K),{a})+\sum_{{x}^{\prime}_{h+1}\in\mathcal{X}_{h+1}}\widetilde{T}(({x}_{h+1}^{\prime},1)\mid({x}_{h},K),{a})\widetilde{Q}(({x}_{h+1}^{\prime},1),{{\widetilde{\pi}}_{\mathrm{e}}}(({x}_{h+1}^{\prime},1)))

\displaystyle=r({x}_{h},{a})+\sum_{{x}_{h+1}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}_{h+1}}T({x}_{h+1}^{\prime}\mid{x}_{h},{a})Q({x}_{h+1}^{\prime},{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}({x}_{h+1}^{\prime}))=Q({x}_{h},{a}).

When
k<K
and
{a}={\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}({x}_{h})
, according to Bellman equation, we have

\displaystyle\widetilde{Q}(({x}_{h},k),{a})

\displaystyle=\widetilde{r}(({x}_{h},k),{a})+\sum_{{x}^{\prime}_{h}\in\mathcal{X}_{h}}\widetilde{T}(({x}_{h}^{\prime},k+1)\mid({x}_{h},k),{a})\widetilde{Q}(({x}_{h}^{\prime},k+1),{{\widetilde{\pi}}_{\mathrm{e}}}(({x}_{h}^{\prime},k+1)))

\displaystyle=\widetilde{Q}(({x}_{h},k+1),{{\widetilde{\pi}}_{\mathrm{e}}}(({x}_{h},k+1)))=Q({x}_{h},{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}({x}_{h})),

where in the second last equation we use the fact that
\widetilde{T}(({x}_{h},k+1)\mid({x}_{h},k),{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}({x}_{h}))=1
.

When
k<K
and
{a}\neq{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}({x}_{h})
, according to Bellman equation, we have

\displaystyle\widetilde{Q}(({x}_{h},k),{a})

\displaystyle=\widetilde{r}(({x}_{h},k),{a})+\sum_{{x}^{\prime}_{h}\in\mathcal{X}_{h}}\widetilde{T}(({x}_{h}^{\prime},k+1)\mid({x}_{h},k),{a})\widetilde{Q}(({x}_{h}^{\prime},k+1),{{\widetilde{\pi}}_{\mathrm{e}}}(({x}_{h}^{\prime},k+1)))

\displaystyle=\sum_{{x}^{\prime}_{h}\in\mathcal{X}_{h}}d_{h}^{\pi_{b}}({x}_{h}^{\prime};M)\widetilde{Q}(({x}_{h}^{\prime},k+1),{{\widetilde{\pi}}_{\mathrm{e}}}(({x}_{h}^{\prime},k+1)))=d^{\pi_{b}}_{h}({x}_{h};M)Q({x}_{h},{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}({x}_{h})),

Finally for
(
43
)
follows from
\widetilde{\rho}(({x}_{1},1))=\rho({x}_{1})
for any
{x}_{1}\in\mathcal{X}_{1}
.

\blacksquare

Lemma 21
.

For a fix OPE problem
\mathfrak{g}=(M,{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}},{\pi_{b}},\mathcal{F})
and parameter
K
, suppose
(\widetilde{M},{{\widetilde{\pi}}_{\mathrm{e}}},{{\widetilde{\pi}}_{b}},\widetilde{\mathcal{F}})
to be the output of
Algorithm
1
with input to be
\mathfrak{g}
and
K
. Suppose
\mathcal{D}^{\textsc{adm}}_{h}
are admissible data collected according to
M
and
{\pi_{b}}
and we feed
\mathcal{D}^{\textsc{adm}}_{h}
into
Algorithm
2
. Let
\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{M}
to be the distribution of the output. Additionally, we suppose the distribution of a trajectory collected from
\widetilde{M}
and
{{\widetilde{\pi}}_{b}}
to be
\mathbb{P}_{\widetilde{M}}
. Let
\mathcal{E}_{0}
to be the following set of trajectories:

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{0}\vcentcolon={}\{\widetilde{\tau}=(\widetilde{{x}}_{1},\widetilde{{a}}_{1},\widetilde{r}_{1},\widetilde{{x}}_{2},\widetilde{{a}}_{2},\widetilde{r}_{2},\cdots,\widetilde{{x}}_{\widetilde{H}})\mid\exists 1\leq h\leq H-1,\widetilde{a}_{(h-1)K+1}=\cdots=\widetilde{a}_{(h-1)K+K-1}=1\}.

(45)

Then for trajectory
\widetilde{\tau}=(\widetilde{{x}}_{1},\widetilde{{a}}_{1},\widetilde{r}_{1},\widetilde{{x}}_{2},\widetilde{{a}}_{2},\widetilde{r}_{2},\cdots,\widetilde{{x}}_{\widetilde{H}})\not\in\mathcal{E}_{0}
, we have

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{M}(\widetilde{\tau})=\mathbb{P}_{\widetilde{M}}(\widetilde{\tau}).

Proof of
Lemma
21
.
We denote
\widetilde{\tau}_{l}=(\widetilde{{x}}_{1},\widetilde{{a}}_{1},\widetilde{r}_{1},\widetilde{{x}}_{2},\widetilde{{a}}_{2},\widetilde{r}_{2},\cdots,\widetilde{{x}}_{l})
to be a partial trajectory till step
l
, and for a trajectory
\widetilde{\tau}
, we say
\widetilde{\tau}\in\widetilde{\tau}_{l}
if the first
l
steps of
\widetilde{\tau}
is exactly
\widetilde{\tau}_{l}
. We denote

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{M}(\widetilde{\tau}_{l})=\sum_{\widetilde{\tau}\in\widetilde{\tau}_{l}}\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{M}(\widetilde{\tau})\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbb{P}_{\widetilde{M}}(\widetilde{\tau})=\sum_{\widetilde{\tau}\in\widetilde{\tau}_{l}}\mathbb{P}_{\widetilde{M}}(\widetilde{\tau}).

We will prove by induction on
l
that

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{M}(\widetilde{\tau}_{l})=\mathbb{P}_{\widetilde{M}}(\widetilde{\tau}_{l}).

(46)

When
l=1
, this is true since
\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{M}(\widetilde{\tau}_{1})=\widetilde{\rho}(\widetilde{{x}}_{1})=\mathbb{P}_{\widetilde{M}}(\widetilde{\tau}_{l})
. Next, to finish induction from
l
to
l+1
, by the chain rule of probability, we only need to show that

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{M}(\widetilde{{a}}_{l},\widetilde{r}_{l},\widetilde{{x}}_{l+1}\mid\widetilde{\tau}_{l})=\mathbb{P}_{\widetilde{M}}(\widetilde{{a}}_{l},\widetilde{r}_{l},\widetilde{{x}}_{l+1}\mid\widetilde{\tau}_{l}).

(47)

We write
l=hK+k
with
1\leq k\leq K
, and
\widetilde{{x}}_{l}=({x}_{h},k)
and
\widetilde{{x}}_{l+1}=({x}_{h}^{\prime},k+1)
(or
({x}_{h+1}^{\prime},1)
). If
k=K
, we have

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{M}(\widetilde{{a}}_{l},\widetilde{r}_{l},\widetilde{{x}}_{l+1}\mid\widetilde{\tau}_{l})={\pi_{b}}(\widetilde{{a}}_{l}\mid{x}_{h})R(\widetilde{r}_{l}\mid{x}_{h},\widetilde{{a}}_{l})T({x}_{h+1}^{\prime}\mid{x}_{h},\widetilde{{a}}_{l}).

According to
Algorithm
2
, as long as there exists some
(h-1)K+1\leq t\leq(h-1)K+K-1
with
\widetilde{a}_{t}\neq 1
, we will have
(\widetilde{{x}}_{l},\widetilde{{a}}_{l},\widetilde{r}_{l},\widetilde{{x}}_{l+1})\in\mathcal{D}^{\textsc{adm}}_{h}
, which indicates that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\widetilde{M}}(\widetilde{{a}}_{l},\widetilde{r}_{l},\widetilde{{x}}_{l+1}\mid\widetilde{\tau}_{l})={\pi_{b}}(\widetilde{{a}}_{l}\mid{x}_{h})R(\widetilde{r}_{l}\mid{x}_{h},\widetilde{{a}}_{l})T({x}_{h+1}^{\prime}\mid{x}_{h},\widetilde{{a}}_{l})=\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{M}(\widetilde{{a}}_{l},\widetilde{r}_{l},\widetilde{{x}}_{l+1}\mid\widetilde{\tau}_{l}).

If
l=hK+k
for some
h
and
1\leq k\leq K-1
, then the transition model of
\widetilde{M}
gives that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\widetilde{M}}(\widetilde{{a}}_{l},\widetilde{r}_{l},\widetilde{{x}}_{l+1}\mid\widetilde{\tau}_{l})=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{2}\cdot\mathbb{I}(\widetilde{r}_{l}=0)\cdot\mathbb{I}({x}_{h}^{\prime}={x}_{h}),&\text{if}\quad\widetilde{{a}}_{l}={\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}({x}_{l}),\\
\frac{1}{2}\cdot\mathbb{I}(\widetilde{r}_{l}=0)\cdot d^{\pi_{b}}_{h}({x}_{h}^{\prime};M),&\text{if}\quad\widetilde{{a}}_{l}={\pi_{c}}({x}_{l}).\end{cases}

Additionally, according to
Algorithm
2
, action
1
is taken with probability
\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}
. Hence if
\widetilde{{a}}_{l}={\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}({x}_{l})
, then the algorithm chooses
{x}_{h}^{\prime}={x}_{h}
and
\widetilde{r}=0
, we have

\displaystyle\text{LHS of }\hyperref@@ii[eq:sec-trajectory-condition-probability]{\textup{(\ref*{eq:sec-trajectory-condition-probability})}}=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\mathbb{I}(\widetilde{r}_{l}=0)\mathbb{I}({x}_{h}^{\prime}={x}_{h}).

And if
\widetilde{{a}}_{l}={\pi_{c}}({x}_{l})
, the algorithm will sample
{x}_{h}^{\prime}
from
d^{\pi_{b}}_{h}(\cdot;M)
. Hence we obtain

\displaystyle\text{LHS of }\hyperref@@ii[eq:sec-trajectory-condition-probability]{\textup{(\ref*{eq:sec-trajectory-condition-probability})}}=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\mathbb{I}(\widetilde{r}_{l}=0)d^{\pi_{b}}_{h}({x}_{h}^{\prime};M).

This finishes the proof of induction at
l+1
.

Finally, by induction,
(
46
)
holds for
l=\widetilde{H}
as long as
\widetilde{\tau}\not\in\mathcal{E}_{0}
. Hence for
\widetilde{\tau}\not\in\mathcal{E}_{0}
we always have
\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{M}(\widetilde{\tau})=\mathbb{P}_{\widetilde{M}}(\widetilde{\tau})
.

\blacksquare

Theorem E.1
.

Suppose for
\widetilde{M}
, algorithm
\textsc{Alg}_{\textsc{traj}}
taking
n
trajectories
\widetilde{\tau}_{1:n}
and state-action value function class
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}
can output
\widehat{V}_{\widetilde{\tau}_{1:n}}\in[-1,1]
such that

\mathbb{E}_{\widetilde{\tau}_{1:n}\sim\widetilde{M}}\left[\left|\widehat{V}_{\widetilde{\tau}_{1:n}}-V^{\widetilde{M}}(\widetilde{\rho})\right|\right]\leq\varepsilon.

Then taking
H^{2}n
admissible dataset
D_{H^{2}n}
and class of tuples of state-action value function together with
W
-function,
Algorithm
3
can output
\varepsilon
-close value to
\widehat{V}_{D_{H^{2}n}}
such that

\mathbb{E}_{\widetilde{\tau}_{1:n}\sim M}\left[\left|\widehat{V}_{D_{n}}-V^{M}(\rho)\right|\right]\leq\varepsilon+H2^{-K+2}.

Proof of
Theorem
E.1
.

We use
\widetilde{\tau}_{1:n}
to denote
n
trajectories
\widetilde{\tau}_{1},\cdots,\widetilde{\tau}_{n}
, and let
e(\widetilde{\tau}_{1:n})=|\widehat{V}_{\widetilde{\tau}_{1:n}}-V^{\widetilde{M}}(\widetilde{\rho})|
. We have

\sum_{\widetilde{\tau}_{1:n}}e(\widetilde{\tau}_{1:n})\prod_{j=1}^{n}\mathbb{P}_{\widetilde{M}}(\widetilde{\tau}_{j})=\mathbb{E}_{\widetilde{\tau}_{1:n}\sim\widetilde{M}}\left[\left|\widehat{V}_{\widetilde{\tau}_{1:n}}-V^{\widetilde{M}}(\widetilde{\rho})\right|\right]\leq\varepsilon.

(48)

We further notice
V(\widetilde{\rho};\widetilde{M})=V(\rho;M)
from
Lemma
20
. Furthermore, since
Algorithm
3
first transit the admissible data
D_{HKn}
into trajectory data
\widetilde{\tau}_{1:n}
according to
Algorithm
2
, and then output
\widehat{V}_{\widetilde{\tau}_{1:n}}
according to algorithm
\textsc{Alg}_{\textsc{traj}}
, we have

\mathbb{E}_{\widetilde{\tau}_{1:n}\sim M}\left[\left|\widehat{V}_{D_{n}}-V^{M}(\rho)\right|\right]=\sum_{\widetilde{\tau}_{1:n}}\left|\widehat{V}_{\widetilde{\tau}_{1:n}}-V^{\widetilde{M}}(\widetilde{\rho})\right|\prod_{j=1}^{n}\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{M}(\widetilde{\tau}_{j})=\sum_{\widetilde{\tau}_{1:n}}e(\widetilde{\tau}_{1:n})\prod_{j=1}^{n}\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{M}(\widetilde{\tau}_{j}),

where
\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{M}
is defined in
Lemma
21
. Next,
Lemma
21
indicates that if
\widetilde{\tau}_{j}\not\in\mathcal{E}_{0}
(where
\mathcal{E}_{0}
is defined in
(
45
)
), then
\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{M}(\widetilde{\tau}_{j})=\mathbb{P}_{\widetilde{M}}(\widetilde{\tau}_{j})
. Therefore, noticing that
e(\widetilde{\tau}_{1:n})=|\widehat{V}_{\widetilde{\tau}_{1:n}}-V^{\widetilde{M}}(\widetilde{\rho})|\leq 2
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{\widetilde{\tau}_{1:n}}e(\widetilde{\tau}_{1:n})\prod_{j=1}^{n}\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{M}(\widetilde{\tau}_{j})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\widetilde{\tau}_{1:n}:\exists 1\leq i\leq n,\widetilde{\tau}_{i}\in\mathcal{E}_{0}}e(\widetilde{\tau}_{1:n})\prod_{j=1}^{n}\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{M}(\widetilde{\tau}_{j})+\sum_{\widetilde{\tau}_{1:n}:\forall 1\leq i\leq n,\widetilde{\tau}_{i}\not\in\mathcal{E}_{0}}e(\widetilde{\tau}_{1:n})\prod_{j=1}^{n}\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{M}(\widetilde{\tau}_{j})

\displaystyle=2\sum_{\widetilde{\tau}_{1:n}:\exists 1\leq i\leq n,\widetilde{\tau}_{i}\in\mathcal{E}_{0}}\prod_{j=1}^{n}\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{M}(\widetilde{\tau}_{j})+\sum_{\widetilde{\tau}_{1:n}:\forall 1\leq i\leq n,\widetilde{\tau}_{i}\not\in\mathcal{E}_{0}}e(\widetilde{\tau}_{1:n})\prod_{j=1}^{n}\mathbb{P}_{\widetilde{M}}(\widetilde{\tau}_{j})

\displaystyle\leq 2\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{M}(\widetilde{\tau}_{i}\in\mathcal{E}_{0})+\sum_{\widetilde{\tau}_{1:n}:\forall 1\leq i\leq n,\widetilde{\tau}_{i}\not\in\mathcal{E}_{0}}e(\widetilde{\tau}_{1:n})\prod_{j=1}^{n}\mathbb{P}_{\widetilde{M}}(\widetilde{\tau}_{j})

\displaystyle\leq 2\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{M}(\widetilde{\tau}_{i}\in\mathcal{E}_{0})+\sum_{\widetilde{\tau}_{1:n}}e(\widetilde{\tau}_{1:n})\prod_{j=1}^{n}\mathbb{P}_{\widetilde{M}}(\widetilde{\tau}_{j})

Further notice that for any
1\leq j\leq n
, we have

\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{M}(\widetilde{\tau}\in\mathcal{E}_{0})=1-(1-2^{-H})^{K}\leq H2^{-K+1}.

Therefore, using
(
48
)
, we obtain

\displaystyle 2\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{M}(\widetilde{\tau}_{i}\in\mathcal{E}_{0})+\sum_{\widetilde{\tau}_{1:n}}e(\widetilde{\tau}_{1:n})\prod_{j=1}^{n}\mathbb{P}_{\widetilde{M}}(\widetilde{\tau}_{j})\leq 2nH2^{-K+1}+\varepsilon,

which indicates that
\mathbb{E}_{\widetilde{\tau}_{1:n}\sim M}\left[\left|\widehat{V}_{D_{n}}-V^{M}(\rho)\right|\right]\leq\varepsilon+H2^{-K+2}
.

\blacksquare

This theorem has the following direct corollary, indicating that any algorithm taking trajectory data as input cannot do policy evaluation in polynomial number of samples.

Proof of
Theorem
4.2
.

According to
Theorem
D.1
, there exists a class
\mathcal{M}
of MDPs, where each
M\in\mathcal{M}
has bounded coverage
384H^{3}
. And any algorithm which takes
o(\nicefrac{{H2^{H}}}{{\varepsilon}})
number of admissible samples together with realizable class of tuples of state-action value function together with
W
-function induces estimation error
\nicefrac{{\varepsilon}}{{8H}}
in at least one MDP. We further carry the lifting in this section for any
MDP
in
\mathcal{M}
, and suppose these lifting MDPs form the class
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}
. Lemma
Lemma
20
indicates that every instance in
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}
has bounded coverage
768H^{3}
.

Next, we will prove this corollary by contradiction. Suppose the algorithm
\textsc{Alg}_{\textsc{traj}}
using
\tilde{o}(\nicefrac{{H2^{H}}}{{\varepsilon}})
trajectories together with realizable state-action value function class induces estimation error less than
\nicefrac{{\varepsilon}}{{16H}}
in every
MDP
in
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}
. Then after inserting
\textsc{Alg}_{\textsc{traj}}
into
Algorithm
3
, we form an algorithm which takes
\tilde{o}(\nicefrac{{H2^{H}}}{{\varepsilon}})
admissible data for each layer, together with realizable function class, and outputs an estimation to the value function.

Theorem
E.1
indicates that this algorithm will induce
\nicefrac{{\varepsilon}}{{16H}}+H2^{-K+2}
error for all MDPs in
\mathcal{M}
. Hence taking
K=2+\log_{2}\nicefrac{{16H^{2}}}{{\varepsilon}}=\log_{2}\nicefrac{{64H^{2}}}{{\varepsilon}}
, this algorithm will induces estimation error less than
\nicefrac{{\varepsilon}}{{8H}}
in all MDPs in
\mathcal{M}
. Notice that with this choice of
K
, we have
\tilde{o}(\nicefrac{{KH2^{H}}}{{\varepsilon}})\leq\tilde{o}(\nicefrac{{H2^{H}}}{{\varepsilon}})
, which contradicts to
Theorem
D.1
.

\blacksquare

Appendix F
Upper Bound for Offline Policy Evaluation

F.1
Setup

In previous sections we construct the lower bound assuming access to the
Q^{{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}}
function class. For the upper bound, we consider a slightly more challenging scenario, where the learner only has access to the
V^{{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}}
function class. It is not hard to see that learning with a
V^{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}
function class is more challenging than learning with
Q^{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}
because one can always reduce a
Q^{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}
function set to a
V
function set by redefining
f({x})\leftarrow\mathbb{E}_{{a}\sim{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}(\cdot\mid{x}))}[f({x},{a})]
.
For simplicity, we assume that
{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}
is deterministic, though the extension to the stochastic case is straightforward. See
Section
F.2
for the discussion.

Similar to
Xie and Jiang 2021
, we first establish results for the case where the function set only
approximately
realizes the true
V^{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}
. The result for the fully realizable setting can be easily deduced from it. Formally, we assume that the learner is given a function set
{\mathcal{F}}
that consists of mappings
{\mathcal{X}}\to[-H,H]
with the following approximate realizability guarantee.

Assumption F.1

(Approximate value function realizability)
.

There exists an
f^{\star}\in{\mathcal{F}}
such that

\sup_{{x}\in{\mathcal{X}}}|V^{{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}}({x})-f^{\star}({x})|\leq\varepsilon_{\textup{appr}}
.

Besides, the learner is given an offline dataset
{\mathcal{D}}
, which consists of
n
tuples of
({x},{a},{r},{x}^{\prime})
with
({x},{a})
drawn from
\mu
, and
r\sim r({x},{a})
and
s^{\prime}\sim T(\cdot\mid{x},{a})
are sampled according to the MDP’s reward function and transition model.

F.2
Algorithm

Algorithm 4

Batch Value-Function Tournament for Policy Evaluation
(
Xie and Jiang 2021
)

1:

Evaluation policy
{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}
, Offline Dataset
\mathcal{D}
consisting of
n
tuples of the form
({x},{a},{r},{x}^{\prime})
.

2:

Among all samples
({x},{a},{r},{x}^{\prime})
in
{\mathcal{D}}
, only keep those such that
a={\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}({x})
, and discard all others. We denote the new dataset as
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}=\{({x}_{i},{r}_{i},{x}_{i}^{\prime})\}_{i=1}^{n^{\prime}}
, where we omit
{a}_{i}
’s since
{a}_{i}
is always equal to

{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}({x}_{i})
in this dataset.

3:

Compute

\displaystyle\widehat{f}=\argmin_{f\in{\mathcal{F}}}\max_{f^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{F}}}\max_{h}\penalty\ \|f-\widehat{{\mathcal{T}}}_{{\mathcal{G}}(f,f^{\prime})}f\|_{\hat{\nu},h}

(49)

where

\displaystyle{\mathcal{G}}(f,f^{\prime})

\displaystyle\vcentcolon={}\bigg\{g:{\mathcal{X}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\penalty\ \bigg|\penalty\ g({x})=g({y})\text{ \ \ if }f({x})=f({y})\text{ and }f^{\prime}({x})=f^{\prime}({y})\text{ and }h({x})=h({y})\bigg\},

(

h({x})\in[H]
denotes the layer at which
{x}
lies

)

\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathcal{T}}}_{\mathcal{G}}f

\displaystyle\vcentcolon={}\argmin_{g\in{\mathcal{G}}}\sum_{i=1}^{n^{\prime}}\left(g({x}_{i})-{r}_{i}-f({x}_{i}^{\prime})\right)^{2},

(50)

\displaystyle\hat{\nu}({x})

\displaystyle\vcentcolon={}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n^{\prime}}\mathbb{I}\{{x}_{i}={x}\}.

(51)

4:

Return

\widehat{f}
.

Our algorithm for this setting is presented in
Algorithm
4
, which is an adaptation of the BVFT algorithm
(
Xie and Jiang 2021
)
to the case of policy evaluation. In the beginning of the algorithm, the dataset
{\mathcal{D}}
is pre-processed so that only
({x},{a},{r},{x}^{\prime})
samples with
{a}={\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}({x})
are kept (
line
2
). The core of the algorithm is to solve the min-max problem in
(
49
)
. The high-level idea of it is that for every pair of functions
f,f^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{F}}
, the algorithm creates a “tabular problem” by aggregating states with the same
(f({x}),f^{\prime}({x}))
value, and estimates the Bellman error for this tabular problem. Intuitively, this is probably the best the learner can do, since besides the value of
(f({x}))_{f\in{\mathcal{F}}}
, the learner has no other ways to distinguish states in the large state space. The output function

\widehat{f}
is the one that always attains a small Bellman error estimate no matter what the other function it is paired with. For more explanation on this min-max formulation, we refer the reader to
Xie and Jiang 2021
or the amazing talk by
Jiang 2021
. The key differences with the algorithm of
Xie and Jiang 2021
are the following:

•

We deal with policy evaluation for
{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}
, and our function class consists of
V^{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}
functions, while
Xie and Jiang 2021
deal with policy optimization, and their function class consists of
Q^{\star}
functions. For this reason, we have a
preprocessing step
in
line
2
of
Algorithm
4
, which removes data samples whose action is not generated by
{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}
. These samples are irrelevant to our policy evaluation task.

•

We consider the finite-horizon setting while
Xie and Jiang 2021
considers the discounted infinite-horizon setting. Therefore, different from theirs, Our aggregation is performed in a layer-by-layer manner, and only states in the same layer can be aggregated.

If
{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}
is stochastic, we perform the pre-processing step (
line
2
) in the following way: for each sample
({x},{a},{r},{x}^{\prime})\in{\mathcal{D}}
, sample
{a}^{\prime}\sim{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}(\cdot\mid{x})
. If
{a}={a}^{\prime}
, then keep this sample; otherwise discard this sample.

Our upper bound result is stated in the following theorem, whose proof is provided in
Appendix
F.3
to
Appendix
F.5
.

Theorem F.1
.

Let
\widehat{f}\in{\mathcal{F}}
be the output of the BVFT algorithm given in
Algorithm
4
. Let
\Phi(f,f^{\prime})
be the state aggregation scheme determined by
f
and
f^{\prime}
(see
Definition
F.2
for the precise definition), and let
\bar{\mathsf{C}}=\max_{f,f^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{F}}}\bar{\mathsf{C}}_{\varepsilon^{2}/H^{2}}(M,\Phi(f,f^{\prime}),\mu)
. For given
\delta>0
, if
n\geq\widetilde{\Omega}\left(\frac{\bar{\mathsf{C}}^{2}H^{6}\log(|{\mathcal{F}}|/\delta)}{\varepsilon^{4}}\right)
, then with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\displaystyle\left\lvert\mathbb{E}_{{x}\sim\rho}[V^{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}({x})-\widehat{f}({x})]\right\rvert\leq O(\varepsilon).

Because of the state aggregation procedure in BVFT, the sample complexity upper bound in
Theorem
F.1
depends on the concentrability coefficient of the aggregated
MDP
(i.e.,
\bar{\mathsf{C}}
) rather than that of the original MDP. Notice also that the sample complexity scales with
\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{4}}
instead of the more common
\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}
. This is similar to
Xie and Jiang 2021
and is because we divide the state space into
O(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}})
aggregations, each of which consists of states having the same value functions up to an accuracy of
\varepsilon
. Our bound have a smaller dependence on the horizon length
H
, but this is simply because we assume the range of the value function is
[-1,1]
while they assume it to be
[-H,H]
.

Finally, we provide some implications of
Theorem
F.1
. First, as pointed out previously, we have
\bar{\mathsf{C}}\leq\mathsf{C}_{\textup{pf}}
(
Lemma
29
). Second, in the case that the data is admissible with offline policy
{\pi_{b}}
, and
\frac{1}{{\pi_{b}}({\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}({x})|{x})}\leq\mathsf{C}_{\mathcal{A}}
for all
{x}
, we have
\bar{\mathsf{C}}\leq(\mathsf{C}_{\mathcal{A}})^{H}
(
Lemma
30
). Interestingly, a sample complexity bound of order
(\mathsf{C}_{\mathcal{A}})^{H}
is also the case if we use
importance sampling
to perform offline policy evaluation. The difference is that importance sampling does not require access to any function class, but needs the data to be trajectories, while
BVFT
requires access to a function class, but only needs the data to be admissible.

For the remaining of this section, we provide a proof for
Theorem
F.1
.

F.3
Definitions

Definition F.1

(partial offline data distribution
\nu({x})
)
.

Given the offline data distribution
\mu\in\Delta({\mathcal{X}}\times{\mathcal{A}})
, we define the partial distribution
\nu\in\Delta({\mathcal{X}})
such that
\nu({x})=\mu({x},{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}({x}))
.

Definition F.2

(aggregation schemes
\Phi(f,f^{\prime})
,
\Phi_{h}(f,f^{\prime})
and maximum partition number
\Phi_{\max}
)
.

Define
\Phi(f,f^{\prime})
as the state aggregation scheme (see
Section
3.1
) where
{x},{y}
belongs to the same partition if and only if
f({x})=f({y})
and
f^{\prime}({x})=f^{\prime}({y})
and
{x},{y}
are in the same layer. Let
\Phi_{h}(f,f^{\prime})\subset\Phi(f,f^{\prime})
be the set of partitions in layer
h
. Define
\Phi_{\max}=\max_{f,f^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{F}}}\max_{h}|\Phi_{h}(f,f^{\prime})|
.

Definition F.3

(aggregation
\Phi^{\star}
, aggregated transition
\bar{T}
, occupancy
\bar{d}
, and offline distribution
\bar{\nu}
)
.

Consider the partition
\Phi^{\star}=\Phi(\widehat{f},f^{\star})
, where
\widehat{f}
is the output of
Algorithm
4
and
f^{\star}
is defined in
Assumption
F.1
. Let
\phi,\phi^{\prime}\in\Phi^{\star}
, Define

\displaystyle\bar{T}(\phi^{\prime}\mid{}\phi)=\frac{\sum_{{x}\in\phi}\sum_{{x}^{\prime}\in\phi^{\prime}}\nu({x})T({x}^{\prime}\mid{}{x},{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}})}{\sum_{{x}\in\phi}\nu({x})}.

Furthermore, let
\bar{d}(\phi)
be the occupancy measure of
{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}
in the aggregated MDP. That is,
\bar{d}
follows the recursive definition below:

\displaystyle\forall\phi^{\prime}\in\Phi^{\star}_{h+1},\quad\bar{d}(\phi^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\phi\in\Phi^{\star}_{h}}\bar{d}(\phi)\bar{T}(\phi^{\prime}\mid{}\phi),\quad\text{with}\ \bar{d}(\phi)=\frac{1}{H}\sum_{{x}\in\phi}\rho({x})\text{\ for\ }\phi\in\Phi^{\star}_{1}.

Also, define
\bar{\nu}(\phi)=\sum_{{x}\in\phi}\nu({x})
.

Definition F.4

(aggregated concentrability
\bar{\mathsf{C}}_{\varepsilon}^{\star}
)
.

The aggregated concentrability with respect to the aggregation
\Phi^{\star}=\Phi(\hat{f},f^{\star})
(defined in
Definition
F.3
) is defined as

\displaystyle\bar{\mathsf{C}}^{\star}_{\varepsilon}=\max_{h}\max\left\{\frac{\sum_{\phi\in{\mathcal{I}}}\bar{d}(\phi)}{\sum_{\phi\in{\mathcal{I}}}\bar{\nu}(\phi)}:\quad{\mathcal{I}}\subset\Phi^{\star}_{h},\quad\sum_{\phi\in{\mathcal{I}}}\bar{d}(\phi)\geq\varepsilon\right\}.

(52)

Definition F.5

(projection operators
{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathcal{G}}f
and
\hat{{\mathcal{T}}}_{\mathcal{G}}f
)
.

Let
f:{\mathcal{X}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, and let
{\mathcal{G}}
be any function set that consists of functions of the form
{\mathcal{X}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
. Define

\displaystyle{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathcal{G}}}f

\displaystyle=\argmin_{g\in{\mathcal{G}}}\sum_{s,{x}^{\prime}}\nu({x})T({x}^{\prime}\mid{}{x},{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}})\left(g({x})-r({x},{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}})-f({x}^{\prime})\right)^{2},

\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathcal{T}}}_{\mathcal{G}}f

\displaystyle=\argmin_{g\in{\mathcal{G}}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n^{\prime}}\left(g({x}_{i})-{r}_{i}-f({x}_{i}^{\prime})\right)^{2}.

Definition F.6

(weighted norm
\|g\|_{w,h}
)
.

Let
g\in{\mathcal{G}}(f,f^{\prime})
and
\Phi=\Phi(f,f^{\prime})
for some
f,f^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{F}}
. Let
w:\Phi\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
be arbitrary. With abuse of notation, define

\|g\|_{w,h}=\sqrt{\sum_{\phi\in\Phi_{h}}w(\phi)g(\phi)^{2}}
,
where
g(\phi)
is such that
g({x})=g(\phi)
for all
{x}\in\phi
.

Definition F.7

(estimation error
\varepsilon_{\textup{stat}}
)
.

\varepsilon_{\textup{stat}}=\sqrt{\frac{\Phi_{\max}\log(n\Phi_{\max}|{\mathcal{F}}|/\delta)}{n}}
, where
n
is the number of offline samples, and
\Phi_{\max}
is defined in
Definition
F.2
.

We next establish a few properties of state aggregation.

Lemma 22
.

Let
g={\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathcal{G}}(f,f^{\prime})}f
for some
f,f^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{F}}
. Fix a layer
h
. Let
g_{\alpha\beta}
be the value of
g({x})
for those
{x}\in{\mathcal{X}}_{h}
’s
such that
f({x})=\alpha
and
f^{\prime}({x})=\beta
. Then
g_{\alpha\beta}
has the following form:

\displaystyle g_{\alpha\beta}=\frac{\sum_{{x}\in{\mathcal{X}}_{h}:f({x})=\alpha,f^{\prime}({x})=\beta}\left(\nu({x}){\mathcal{T}}f({x})\right)}{\sum_{{x}\in{\mathcal{X}}_{h}:f({x})=\alpha,f^{\prime}({x})=\beta}\left(\nu({x})\right)}.

Proof of
Lemma
22
.

Recall from
Definition
F.5
that
g
is the minimizer of
\mathbb{E}_{{x}\sim\nu}\mathbb{E}_{{x}^{\prime}\sim T(\cdot\mid{}{x},{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}})}\left(g({x})-r({x},{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}})-f({x}^{\prime})\right)^{2}
within
{\mathcal{G}}(f,f^{\prime})
. The derivative of this objective with respect to
g_{\alpha\beta}
is

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{{x}\sim\nu}\mathbb{E}_{{x}^{\prime}\sim T(\cdot\mid{}{x},{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}})}2(g_{\alpha\beta}-r({x},{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}})-f({x}^{\prime}))\mathbb{I}\{f({x})=\alpha,f^{\prime}({x})=\beta,h({x})=h\}

\displaystyle=2g_{\alpha\beta}\sum_{{x}\in{\mathcal{X}}_{h}:\penalty\ f({x})=\alpha,f^{\prime}({x})=\beta}\nu({x})-2\sum_{{x}\in{\mathcal{X}}_{h}:f({x})=\alpha,f^{\prime}({x})=\beta}\nu({x})\left(r({x},{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}})+\sum_{{x}^{\prime}}T({x}^{\prime}\mid{}{x},{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}})f({x}^{\prime})\right)

\displaystyle=2g_{\alpha\beta}\sum_{{x}\in{\mathcal{X}}_{h}:\penalty\ f({x})=\alpha,f^{\prime}({x})=\beta}\nu({x})-2\sum_{{x}\in{\mathcal{X}}_{h}:f({x})=\alpha,f^{\prime}({x})=\beta}\nu({x}){\mathcal{T}}f({x}).

Setting this to be zero gives the desired expression of
g_{\alpha\beta}
.

\blacksquare

Lemma 23
.

For any
f\in{\mathcal{F}}
, we have that
\max_{f^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{F}}}\|f-{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathcal{G}}(f,f^{\prime})}f\|_{\nu,h}\leq\|f-{\mathcal{T}}f\|_{\nu,h}
.

Proof of
Lemma
23
.

Fix
f,f^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{F}}
and fix
h\in[H]
. Let
g={\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathcal{G}}(f,f^{\prime})}f
and let
g_{\alpha\beta}
be the value of
g({x})
for
{x}\in{\mathcal{X}}_{h}
such that
f({x})=\alpha
and
f^{\prime}({x})=\beta
.

Define
\nu_{\alpha\beta}=\sum_{{x}\in{\mathcal{X}}_{h}}\nu({x})\mathbb{I}\{f({x})=\alpha\text{\ and\ }f^{\prime}({x})=\beta\}
. Then by definition, we have

\displaystyle\|f-{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathcal{G}}(f,f^{\prime})}f\|_{\nu,h}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\alpha,\beta}\nu_{\alpha\beta}(\alpha-g_{\alpha\beta})^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\alpha,\beta}\nu_{\alpha\beta}\left(\alpha-\frac{1}{\nu_{\alpha\beta}}\sum_{{x}\in{\mathcal{X}}_{h}:f({x})=\alpha,f^{\prime}({x})=\beta}\nu({x}){\mathcal{T}}f({x})\right)^{2}

(using
Lemma
22
)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\alpha,\beta}\nu_{\alpha\beta}\frac{1}{\nu_{\alpha\beta}}\sum_{{x}\in{\mathcal{X}}_{h}:f({x})=\alpha,f^{\prime}({x})=\beta}\nu({x})\left(\alpha-{\mathcal{T}}f({x})\right)^{2}

(Jensen’s inequality)

\displaystyle=\sum_{{x}\in{\mathcal{X}}_{h}}\nu({x})\left(f({x})-{\mathcal{T}}f({x})\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\|f-{\mathcal{T}}f\|_{\nu,h}^{2}.

\blacksquare

F.4
Supporting Technical Results

Lemma 24
.

With probability at least
1-\delta
, for all
f,f^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{F}}
and all
g\in{\mathcal{G}}(f,f^{\prime})
such that
\sup_{{x}}|g({x})|\leq 1
, it holds that

\displaystyle\|g\|_{\nu,h}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{2}\|g\|_{\hat{\nu},h}+O\left(\varepsilon_{\textup{stat}}\right),

\displaystyle\|g\|_{\hat{\nu},h}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{2}\|g\|_{\nu,h}+O\left(\varepsilon_{\textup{stat}}\right).

(Recall the definition of
\varepsilon_{\textup{stat}}
in
Definition
F.7
)

Proof of
Lemma
24
.

Fix
f,f^{\prime}
and
g
.
By Bernstein’s inequality, with probability at least
1-\delta^{\prime}
,

\displaystyle\left|\|g\|_{\nu,h}^{2}-\|g\|_{\hat{\nu},h}^{2}\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n^{\prime}}\left(g({x}_{i})^{2}-\sum_{s}\nu({x})g({x})^{2}\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq O\left(\sqrt{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{s}\nu({x})g({x})^{4}\log(1/\delta^{\prime})}+\frac{\log(1/\delta^{\prime})}{n}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{s}\nu({x})g({x})^{2}+O\left(\frac{\log(1/\delta^{\prime})}{n}\right)

(AM-GM)

\displaystyle=\|g\|_{\nu,h}^{2}+O\left(\frac{\log(1/\delta^{\prime})}{n}\right).

Rearranging this gives

\displaystyle\|g\|^{2}_{\nu,h}\leq 2\|g\|^{2}_{\hat{\nu},h}+O\left(\frac{\log(1/\delta^{\prime})}{n}\right)\qquad\text{and}\qquad\|g\|^{2}_{\hat{\nu},h}\leq 2\|g\|^{2}_{\nu,h}+O\left(\frac{\log(1/\delta^{\prime})}{n}\right).

Next, we take union bounds over
f,f^{\prime}
and
g
. Notice that for every pair of
(f,f^{\prime})
, the value of
g\in{\mathcal{G}}(f,f^{\prime})
on
x\in{\mathcal{X}}_{h}
is determined by the values of
\{g(\phi)\}_{\phi\in\Phi_{h}(f,f^{\prime})}
, where
g(\phi)
is the value of
g({x})
for
x\in\phi
. Therefore, an
\varepsilon
-net of
{\mathcal{G}}(f,f^{\prime})
on layer
h
can be constructed by discretizing each value of
\{g(\phi)\}_{\phi\in\Phi_{h}(f,f^{\prime})}
, and its size is at most
(1/\varepsilon)^{O(|\Phi_{h}(f,f^{\prime})|)}\leq(1/\varepsilon)^{O(\Phi_{\max})}
. It suffices to pick
\varepsilon=\frac{1}{n}
and bound the discretization error by
O(\frac{1}{n})
. Overall, the union bound is taken over
|{\mathcal{F}}|^{2}n^{\Phi_{\max}}
instances. Therefore, we pick
\delta^{\prime}=\frac{\delta}{|{\mathcal{F}}|^{2}n^{\Phi_{\max}}}
, which gives

\displaystyle\|g\|^{2}_{\nu,h}

\displaystyle\leq 2|g\|^{2}_{\hat{\nu},h}+O\left(\frac{\Phi_{\max}\log(n|{\mathcal{F}}|/\delta)}{n}\right),\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle\|g\|^{2}_{\hat{\nu},h}

\displaystyle\leq 2\|g\|^{2}_{\nu,h}+O\left(\frac{\Phi_{\max}\log(n|{\mathcal{F}}|/\delta)}{n}\right).

Finally, taking square root on both sides and recalling that
\varepsilon_{\textup{stat}}=\sqrt{\frac{\Phi_{\max}\log(n\Phi_{\max}|{\mathcal{F}}|/\delta)}{n}}
finishes the proof.

\blacksquare

Lemma 25
.

With probability at least
1-\delta
, for all
f,f^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{F}}
, and
h\leq H
,

\displaystyle\|{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathcal{G}}(f,f^{\prime})}f-\widehat{{\mathcal{T}}}_{{\mathcal{G}}(f,f^{\prime})}f\|_{\nu,h}\leq\mathcal{O}\left(\varepsilon_{\textup{stat}}\right).

Proof of
Lemma
25
.

We first fix the function pair
f,f^{\prime}
and define additional notation. Let
\Phi=\Phi(f,f^{\prime})
and
{\mathcal{G}}={\mathcal{G}}(f,f^{\prime})
. For every
\phi\in\Phi_{h}
, define

\displaystyle Y(\phi)

\displaystyle=\sum_{{x}\in\phi}\nu({x})\left(r({x},{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}})+\sum_{{x}^{\prime}}T({x}^{\prime}\mid{}{x},{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}})f({x}^{\prime})\right),\ \

\displaystyle Z(\phi)=\sum_{{x}\in\phi}\nu({x}),

\displaystyle\widehat{Y}(\phi)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n^{\prime}}\mathbb{I}\{{x}_{i}\in\phi\}\left(r({x}_{i},{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}})+f({x}_{i}^{\prime})\right),

\displaystyle\widehat{Z}(\phi)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n^{\prime}}\mathbb{I}\{{x}_{i}\in\phi\}.

Additionally, let
\delta^{\prime}=\frac{\delta}{|{\mathcal{F}}|^{2}\Phi_{\max}}
,
\varepsilon_{0}=\frac{\log(1/\delta^{\prime})}{n}
, and
\Phi_{h}^{\prime}=\{\phi\in\Phi_{h}:\penalty\ Z(\phi)\geq\varepsilon_{0}\}
.

Using
Lemma
22
together with definitions above, we get that for all
{x}\in\phi
,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathcal{G}}}f({x})=\frac{Y(\phi)}{Z(\phi)}\quad\text{and}\quad\widehat{{\mathcal{T}}}_{{\mathcal{G}}}f({x})=\frac{\widehat{Y}(\phi)}{\widehat{Z}(\phi)}.

Thus, we have

\displaystyle\|{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathcal{G}}f-\widehat{{\mathcal{T}}}_{\mathcal{G}}f\|_{\nu,h}^{2}

(53)

\displaystyle=\sum_{{x}\in{\mathcal{X}}_{h}}\nu({x})\left({\mathcal{T}}_{\mathcal{G}}f({x})-\widehat{{\mathcal{T}}}_{\mathcal{G}}f({x})\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\phi\in\Phi_{h}}\sum_{{x}\in\phi}\nu({x})\left(\frac{Y(\phi)}{Z(\phi)}-\frac{\widehat{Y}(\phi)}{\widehat{Z}(\phi)}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\phi\in\Phi_{h}^{\prime}}\sum_{{x}\in\phi}\nu({x})\left(\frac{Y(\phi)}{Z(\phi)}-\frac{\widehat{Y}(\phi)}{\widehat{Z}(\phi)}\right)^{2}+4\sum_{\phi\in\Phi_{h}\setminus\Phi_{h}^{\prime}}\sum_{{x}\in\phi}\nu({x})H^{2}

(
\frac{Y(\phi)}{Z(\phi)}-\frac{\widehat{Y}(\phi)}{\widehat{Z}(\phi)}\in[-2,2]
)

\displaystyle\leq 2\sum_{\phi\in\Phi_{h}^{\prime}}Z(\phi)\left(\frac{Y(\phi)}{Z(\phi)}-\frac{\widehat{Y}(\phi)}{Z(\phi)}\right)^{2}+2\sum_{\phi\in\Phi_{h}^{\prime}}Z(\phi)\left(\frac{\widehat{Y}(\phi)}{Z(\phi)}-\frac{\widehat{Y}(\phi)}{\widehat{Z}(\phi)}\right)^{2}+4\sum_{\phi\in\Phi_{h}\setminus\Phi_{h}^{\prime}}Z(\phi)

(54)

\displaystyle\leq 2\sum_{\phi\in\Phi_{h}^{\prime}}\frac{(Y(\phi)-\widehat{Y}(\phi))^{2}}{Z(\phi)}+2\sum_{\phi\in\Phi_{h}^{\prime}}\frac{\widehat{Y}(\phi)^{2}}{\widehat{Z}(\phi)^{2}}\frac{(Z(\phi)-\widehat{Z}(\phi))^{2}}{Z(\phi)}+4|\Phi_{h}|\varepsilon_{0}

\displaystyle\leq 2\sum_{\phi\in\Phi_{h}^{\prime}}\frac{(Y(\phi)-\widehat{Y}(\phi))^{2}+(Z(\phi)-\widehat{Z}(\phi))^{2}}{Z(\phi)}+\frac{4\Phi_{\max}\log(1/\delta^{\prime})}{n},

(55)

where the last line follows by observing that
\nicefrac{{\widehat{Y}(\phi)}}{{\widehat{Z}(\phi)}}\leq 1
for any
\phi\in\Phi
.

Next, using Bernstein’s inequality for any
\phi\in\Phi_{h}
, with probability at least
1-\delta^{\prime}
, we have

\displaystyle|Y(\phi)-\widehat{Y}(\phi)|

\displaystyle\leq O\left(\sqrt{\frac{Z(\phi)}{n}\log(1/\delta^{\prime})}+\frac{\log(1/\delta^{\prime})}{n}\right),

\displaystyle|Z(\phi)-\widehat{Z}(\phi)|

\displaystyle\leq O\left(\sqrt{\frac{Z(\phi)}{n}\log(1/\delta^{\prime})}+\frac{\log(1/\delta^{\prime})}{n}\right).

Plugging the above in
(
55
)
, and using a union bound over
\phi\in\Phi_{h}^{\prime}
, we get that with probability at least
1-\delta^{\prime}\Phi_{\max}
,

\displaystyle\|{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathcal{G}}f-\widehat{{\mathcal{T}}}_{\mathcal{G}}f\|_{\nu,h}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{O}\left(\sum_{\phi\in\Phi_{h}^{\prime}}\frac{\log(1/\delta^{\prime})}{n}+\frac{\log^{2}(1/\delta^{\prime})}{n^{2}Z(\phi)}+\frac{\Phi_{\max}\log(1/\delta^{\prime})}{n}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\Phi_{\max}\log(1/\delta^{\prime})}{n}+\frac{\Phi_{\max}\log^{2}(1/\delta^{\prime})}{n^{2}\varepsilon_{0}}+\frac{\Phi_{\max}\log(1/\delta^{\prime})}{n}\right)

(for
\phi\in\Phi_{h}^{\prime}
,
Z(\phi)\geq\varepsilon_{0}
)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\Phi_{\max}\log(|{\mathcal{F}}|\Phi_{\max}/\delta)}{n}\right).

(by the definition of
\varepsilon_{0}
and
\delta^{\prime}
)

Finally, using a union bound over
(f,f^{\prime})\in{\mathcal{F}}\times{\mathcal{F}}
and recalling that
\varepsilon_{\textup{stat}}=\sqrt{\frac{\Phi_{\max}\log(n|{\mathcal{F}}|\Phi_{\max}/\delta)}{n}}
finishes the proof.

\blacksquare

Lemma 26
.

Let
\widehat{f}
be the output of
Algorithm
4
and
f^{\star}
be defined in
Assumption
F.1
.

(a)

\|\widehat{f}-{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathcal{G}}(\widehat{f},f^{\star})}\widehat{f}\|_{\nu,h}\leq\mathcal{O}\left(\varepsilon_{\textup{appr}}+\varepsilon_{\textup{stat}}\right)
.

(b)

\|{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathcal{G}}(\widehat{f},f^{\star})}\widehat{f}-{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathcal{G}}(\widehat{f},f^{\star})}f^{\star}\|_{\bar{d},h}\leq\|\widehat{f}-f^{\star}\|_{\bar{d},h+1}
.

(recall the definition of
\bar{d}
in
Definition
F.3
)

Proof of
Lemma
26
.

We prove the two parts separately below.

(a)

Note that

\displaystyle\hskip-21.68121pt\max_{h}\|\widehat{f}-{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathcal{G}}(\widehat{f},f^{\star})}\widehat{f}\|_{\nu,h}

\displaystyle\leq\max_{h}\|\widehat{f}-\widehat{{\mathcal{T}}}_{{\mathcal{G}}(\widehat{f},f^{\star})}\widehat{f}\|_{\nu,h}+\|\widehat{{\mathcal{T}}}_{{\mathcal{G}}(\widehat{f},f^{\star})}\widehat{f}-{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathcal{G}}(\widehat{f},f^{\star})}\widehat{f}\|_{\nu,h}

(triangle inequality)

\displaystyle\leq\max_{f^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{F}}}\max_{h}\|\widehat{f}-\widehat{{\mathcal{T}}}_{{\mathcal{G}}(\hat{f},f^{\prime})}\widehat{f}\|_{\nu,h}+O\left(\varepsilon_{\textup{stat}}\right)

(by Lemma
25
)

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{2}\max_{f^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{F}}}\max_{h}\|\widehat{f}-\widehat{{\mathcal{T}}}_{{\mathcal{G}}(\hat{f},f^{\prime})}\widehat{f}\|_{\hat{\nu},h}+O\left(\varepsilon_{\textup{stat}}\right)

(by Lemma
24
)

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{2}\max_{f^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{F}}}\max_{h}\|f^{\star}-\widehat{{\mathcal{T}}}_{{\mathcal{G}}(f^{\star},f^{\prime})}f^{\star}\|_{\hat{\nu},h}+O\left(\varepsilon_{\textup{stat}}\right)

(by the choice of
\widehat{f}
in
(
49
)
)

\displaystyle\leq 2\max_{f^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{F}}}\max_{h}\|f^{\star}-\widehat{{\mathcal{T}}}_{{\mathcal{G}}(f^{\star},f^{\prime})}f^{\star}\|_{\nu,h}+O\left(\varepsilon_{\textup{stat}}\right)

(by Lemma
24
)

\displaystyle\leq 2\max_{f^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{F}}}\max_{h}\|f^{\star}-{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathcal{G}}(f^{\star},f^{\prime})}f^{\star}\|_{\nu,h}+O\left(\varepsilon_{\textup{stat}}\right)

(by Lemma
25
)

\displaystyle\leq 2\max_{h}\|f^{\star}-{\mathcal{T}}f^{\star}\|_{\nu,h}+O\left(\varepsilon_{\textup{stat}}\right).

(by Lemma
23
)

(b)

For the ease of notation, let
{\mathcal{G}}={\mathcal{G}}(\widehat{f},f^{\star})
. Notice that by
Lemma
22
,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathcal{G}}}\widehat{f}(\phi)-{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathcal{G}}}f^{\star}(\phi)

\displaystyle=\frac{\sum_{{x}\in\phi}\nu({x})\sum_{{x}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{X}}_{h+1}}T({x}^{\prime}\mid{}{x},{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}})\left(\widehat{f}({x}^{\prime})-f^{\star}({x}^{\prime})\right)}{\sum_{{x}\in\phi}\nu({x})}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\|{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathcal{G}}}\widehat{f}-{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathcal{G}}}f^{\star}\|^{2}_{\bar{d},h}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\phi\in\Phi_{h}}\bar{d}(\phi)\left({\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathcal{G}}}\widehat{f}(\phi)-{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathcal{G}}}f^{\star}(\phi)\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\phi\in\Phi_{h}}\bar{d}(\phi)\left(\frac{\sum_{s\in\phi}\nu({x})\sum_{{x}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{X}}_{h+1}}T({x}^{\prime}\mid{}{x},{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}})\left(\widehat{f}({x}^{\prime})-f^{\star}({x}^{\prime})\right)}{\sum_{s\in\phi}\nu({x})}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{{x}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{X}}_{h+1}}\sum_{\phi\in\Phi_{h}}\bar{d}(\phi)\left(\frac{\sum_{s\in\phi}\nu({x})T({x}^{\prime}\mid{}{x},{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}})}{\sum_{s\in\phi}\nu({x})}\right)\left(\widehat{f}({x}^{\prime})-f^{\star}({x}^{\prime})\right)^{2}

(Jensen’s inequality)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\phi^{\prime}\in\Phi_{h+1}}\sum_{{x}^{\prime}\in\phi^{\prime}}\sum_{\phi\in\Phi_{h}}\bar{d}(\phi)\left(\frac{\sum_{s\in\phi}\nu({x})T({x}^{\prime}\mid{}{x},{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}})}{\sum_{s\in\phi}\nu({x})}\right)\left(\widehat{f}(\phi^{\prime})-f^{\star}(\phi^{\prime})\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\phi^{\prime}\in\Phi_{h+1}}\sum_{\phi\in\Phi_{h}}\bar{d}(\phi)\bar{T}(\phi^{\prime}\mid{}\phi)\left(\widehat{f}(\phi^{\prime})-f^{\star}(\phi^{\prime})\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\phi^{\prime}\in\Phi_{h+1}}\bar{d}(\phi^{\prime})\left(\widehat{f}(\phi^{\prime})-f^{\star}(\phi^{\prime})\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\|\widehat{f}-f^{\star}\|_{\bar{d},h+1}^{2}.

\blacksquare

Lemma 27
.

With the
\Phi^{\star}
and
\bar{\mathsf{C}}^{\star}_{\varepsilon}
defined in
Definition
F.3
and
Definition
F.4
, there exist
{\mathcal{J}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathcal{J}}_{H}
such that

•

{\mathcal{J}}_{h}\subset\Phi^{\star}_{h}

•

\sum_{\phi\in{\mathcal{J}}_{h}}\bar{d}(\phi)<\varepsilon

•

\max_{h}\max_{\phi\in\Phi_{h}\setminus{\mathcal{J}}_{h}}\frac{\bar{d}(\phi)}{\bar{\nu}(\phi)}\leq\bar{\mathsf{C}}^{\star}_{\varepsilon}

Proof of
Lemma
27
.

Define

\displaystyle{\mathcal{I}}_{h}=\argmax\left\{\frac{\sum_{\phi\in{\mathcal{I}}}\bar{d}(\phi)}{\sum_{\phi\in{\mathcal{I}}}\bar{\nu}(\phi)}:\quad{\mathcal{I}}\subset\Phi^{\star}_{h},\quad\sum_{\phi\in{\mathcal{I}}}\bar{d}(\phi)\geq\varepsilon\right\}.

(56)

If
{\mathcal{I}}_{h}
has more than one solution, we pick one such that
|{\mathcal{I}}_{h}|
is the smallest.
By the definition of
\bar{\mathsf{C}}^{\star}_{\varepsilon}
, we know that
\frac{\sum_{\phi\in{\mathcal{I}}_{h}}\bar{d}(\phi)}{\sum_{\phi\in{\mathcal{I}}_{h}}\bar{\nu}(\phi)}\leq\bar{\mathsf{C}}^{\star}_{\varepsilon}
for all
h
.

Assume
{\mathcal{I}}_{h}=\{\phi_{h,1},\ldots,\phi_{h,N_{h}}\}
where
N_{h}=|{\mathcal{I}}_{h}|
, and assume without loss of generality that

\frac{\bar{d}(\phi_{h,1})}{\bar{\nu}(\phi_{h,1})}\geq\frac{\bar{d}(\phi_{h,2})}{\bar{\nu}(\phi_{h,2})}\geq\cdots\geq\frac{\bar{d}(\phi_{h,N_{h}})}{\bar{\nu}(\phi_{h,N_{h}})}.

If
N_{h}=1
, it is easy to see that
\phi_{h,1}=\argmax_{\phi\in\Phi^{\star}_{h}}\frac{\bar{d}(\phi)}{\bar{\nu}(\phi)}
. This is because if not, then

\left\{\phi_{h,1},\ \argmax_{\phi\in\Phi^{\star}_{h}}\frac{\bar{d}(\phi)}{\bar{\nu}(\phi)}\right\}

will be a better solution for
{\mathcal{I}}_{h}
in
(
56
)
than
\{\phi_{h,1}\}
, contradicting that
{\mathcal{I}}_{h}=\{\phi_{h,1}\}
. Thus, in this case,
{\mathcal{I}}_{h}=\left\{\argmax_{\phi\in\Phi^{\star}_{h}}\frac{\bar{d}(\phi)}{\bar{\nu}(\phi)}\right\}
. Then choosing
{\mathcal{J}}_{h}=\emptyset
satisfies all conditions in the lemma.

If
N_{h}\geq 2
, we define
{\mathcal{J}}_{h}={\mathcal{I}}_{h}\setminus\{\phi_{h,N_{h}}\}=\{\phi_{h,1},\ldots,\phi_{h,N_{h}-1}\}
. Below we verify that it satisfies the two inequalities in the lemma.

First, we prove
\sum_{\phi\in{\mathcal{J}}_{h}}\bar{d}(\phi)<\varepsilon
by contradiction. Suppose that
\sum_{\phi\in{\mathcal{J}}_{h}}\bar{d}(\phi)\geq\varepsilon
. By the assumption that

\frac{\bar{d}(\phi_{h,1})}{\bar{\nu}(\phi_{h,1})}\geq\cdots\geq\frac{\bar{d}(\phi_{h,N_{h}})}{\bar{\nu}(\phi_{h,N_{h}})},

we have

\frac{\sum_{\phi\in{\mathcal{J}}_{h}}\bar{d}(\phi)}{\sum_{\phi\in{\mathcal{J}}_{h}}\bar{\nu}(\phi)}\geq\frac{\sum_{\phi\in{\mathcal{I}}_{h}}\bar{d}(\phi)}{\sum_{\phi\in{\mathcal{I}}_{h}}\bar{\nu}(\phi)},

and thus
{\mathcal{J}}_{h}
is also a solution of
(
56
)
. However,
|{\mathcal{J}}_{h}|<|{\mathcal{I}}_{h}|
, contradicting the assumption that
|{\mathcal{I}}_{h}|
is the smallest.

Next, we prove
\max_{h}\max_{\phi\in\Phi^{\star}_{h}\setminus{\mathcal{J}}_{h}}\frac{\bar{d}(\phi)}{\bar{\nu}(\phi)}\leq\bar{\mathsf{C}}^{\star}_{\varepsilon}
. Define

\phi_{h}^{\prime}:=\argmax_{\phi\in\Phi^{\star}_{h}\setminus{\mathcal{J}}_{h}}\frac{\bar{d}(\phi)}{\bar{\nu}(\phi)}.

If
\phi_{h}^{\prime}=\phi_{h,N_{h}}
, then we have

\frac{\bar{d}(\phi_{h}^{\prime})}{\bar{\nu}(\phi_{h}^{\prime})}=\frac{\bar{d}(\phi_{h,N_{h}})}{\bar{\nu}(\phi_{h,N_{h}})}\leq\frac{\sum_{\phi\in{\mathcal{I}}_{h}}\bar{d}(\phi)}{\sum_{\phi\in{\mathcal{I}}_{h}}\bar{\nu}(\phi)}\leq\bar{\mathsf{C}}^{\star}_{\varepsilon}.

If
\phi_{h}^{\prime}\neq\phi_{h,N_{h}}
and
\frac{\bar{d}(\phi_{h}^{\prime})}{\bar{\nu}(\phi_{h}^{\prime})}>\bar{\mathsf{C}}^{\star}_{\varepsilon}
, then we have

\frac{\sum_{\phi\in{\mathcal{I}}_{h}}\bar{d}(\phi)+\bar{d}(\phi_{h}^{\prime})}{\sum_{\phi\in{\mathcal{I}}_{h}}\bar{\nu}(\phi)+\bar{\mu}(\phi_{h}^{\prime})}>\frac{\sum_{\phi\in{\mathcal{I}}_{h}}\bar{d}(\phi)}{\sum_{\phi\in{\mathcal{I}}_{h}}\bar{\nu}(\phi)}

because
\frac{\sum_{\phi\in{\mathcal{I}}_{h}}\bar{d}(\phi)}{\sum_{\phi\in{\mathcal{I}}_{h}}\bar{\nu}(\phi)}\leq\bar{\mathsf{C}}^{\star}_{\varepsilon}
. This implies that
{\mathcal{I}}_{h}\cup\{\phi_{h}^{\prime}\}
is a better solution than
{\mathcal{I}}_{h}
in
(
56
)
, which is a contradiction. This concludes the proof.
\blacksquare

F.5
Proof of
Theorem
5.1

Lemma 28
.

Let
\bar{\mathsf{C}}^{\star}=\bar{\mathsf{C}}^{\star}_{\varepsilon^{2}/H^{2}}
. With probability
\geq 1-\delta
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{{x}\sim\rho}[|\widehat{f}({x})-f^{\star}({x})|]\leq\mathcal{O}\left(H\varepsilon_{\textup{appr}}\sqrt{\bar{\mathsf{C}}^{\star}}+H\sqrt{\frac{\bar{\mathsf{C}}^{\star}\Phi_{\max}\log(n\Phi_{\max}|{\mathcal{F}}|/\delta)}{n}}+\varepsilon\right).

Proof of
Lemma
28
.

In this proof, we denote
{\mathcal{G}}={\mathcal{G}}(\widehat{f},f^{\star})
. Recall the definitions of
\Phi^{\star}
,
\bar{d}
, and
\bar{\nu}
in
Definition
F.3
. Notice that
\widehat{f},f^{\star},{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathcal{G}}\widehat{f},{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathcal{G}}f^{\star}\in{\mathcal{G}}
. For any
g\in{\mathcal{G}}
and
\phi\in\Phi^{\star}
, we use
g(\phi)
to represent the value of
g({x})
for those
{x}\in\phi
.
Using
Definition
F.6
, we have

\displaystyle\|\widehat{f}-f^{\star}\|_{\bar{d},h}

\displaystyle\leq\|f^{\star}-{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathcal{G}}}f^{\star}\|_{\bar{d},h}+\|\widehat{f}-{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathcal{G}}}\widehat{f}\|_{\bar{d},h}+\|{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathcal{G}}}\widehat{f}-{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathcal{G}}}f^{\star}\|_{\bar{d},h}.

(57)

Before bounding the three terms above, we notice that by
Lemma
27
, there exist
\{{\mathcal{J}}_{h}\}_{h\in[H]}
such that

•

{\mathcal{J}}_{h}\subset\Phi^{\star}_{h}

•

\sum_{\phi\in{\mathcal{J}}_{h}}\bar{d}(\phi)<\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{H^{2}}

•

\max_{h}\max_{\phi\in\Phi^{\star}_{h}\setminus{\mathcal{J}}_{h}}\frac{\bar{d}(\phi)}{\bar{\nu}(\phi)}\leq\bar{\mathsf{C}}^{\star}_{\varepsilon^{2}/H^{2}}

Below we denote
\bar{\mathsf{C}}^{\star}=\bar{\mathsf{C}}^{\star}_{\varepsilon^{2}/H^{2}}
.
Now we bound the three terms in
(
57
)
. The square of the first term can be upper bounded as

\displaystyle\|f^{\star}-{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathcal{G}}}f^{\star}\|_{\bar{d},h}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\phi\in\Phi^{\star}_{h}}\bar{d}(\phi)\left(f^{\star}(\phi)-{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathcal{G}}f^{\star}(\phi)\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\phi\in\Phi^{\star}_{h}\setminus{\mathcal{J}}_{h}}\bar{d}(\phi)\left(f^{\star}(\phi)-{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathcal{G}}f^{\star}(\phi)\right)^{2}+\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{H^{2}}

(
\sum_{\phi\in{\mathcal{J}}_{h}}\bar{d}(\phi)\leq\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{H^{2}}
)

\displaystyle\leq\bar{\mathsf{C}}^{\star}\sum_{\phi\in\Phi^{\star}_{h}\setminus{\mathcal{J}}_{h}}\bar{\nu}(\phi)\left(f^{\star}(\phi)-{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathcal{G}}f^{\star}(\phi)\right)^{2}+\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{H^{2}}

(by the definition of
\bar{\mathsf{C}}
)

\displaystyle\leq\bar{\mathsf{C}}^{\star}\|f^{\star}-{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathcal{G}}}f^{\star}\|_{\bar{\nu},h}^{2}+\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{H^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\bar{\mathsf{C}}^{\star}\|f^{\star}-{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathcal{G}}}f^{\star}\|^{2}_{\nu,h}+\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{H^{2}}

(because
f^{\star},{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathcal{G}}f^{\star}\in{\mathcal{G}}
)

\displaystyle\leq\bar{\mathsf{C}}\|f^{\star}-{\mathcal{T}}f^{\star}\|_{\nu,h}^{2}+\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{H^{2}}

(by Lemma
23
)

\displaystyle\leq\bar{\mathsf{C}}^{\star}\varepsilon_{\textup{appr}}^{2}+\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{H^{2}}.

(by the definition of
\varepsilon_{\textup{appr}}
)

The square of the second term can be upper bounded as

\displaystyle\|\widehat{f}-{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathcal{G}}}\widehat{f}\|^{2}_{\bar{d},h}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\phi\in\Phi^{\star}_{h}}\bar{d}(\phi)\left(\widehat{f}(\phi)-{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathcal{G}}}\widehat{f}(\phi)\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\phi\in\Phi^{\star}_{h}\setminus{\mathcal{J}}_{h}}\bar{d}(\phi)\left(\widehat{f}(\phi)-{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathcal{G}}}\widehat{f}(\phi)\right)^{2}+\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{H^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\bar{\mathsf{C}}^{\star}\sum_{\phi\in\Phi^{\star}_{h}\setminus{\mathcal{J}}_{h}}\bar{\nu}(\phi)\left(\widehat{f}(\phi)-{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathcal{G}}}\widehat{f}(\phi)\right)^{2}+\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{H^{2}}

(by the definition of
\bar{\mathsf{C}}^{\star}
)

\displaystyle\leq\bar{\mathsf{C}}^{\star}\|\widehat{f}-{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathcal{G}}}\widehat{f}\|_{\bar{\nu},h}^{2}+\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{H^{2}}

\displaystyle=\bar{\mathsf{C}}^{\star}\|\widehat{f}-{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathcal{G}}}\widehat{f}\|_{\nu,h}^{2}+\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{H^{2}}

(because
\widehat{f},{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathcal{G}}\widehat{f}\in{\mathcal{G}}
)

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{O}\left(\bar{\mathsf{C}}^{\star}\varepsilon_{\textup{appr}}^{2}+\bar{\mathsf{C}}^{\star}\varepsilon_{\textup{stat}}^{2}\right)+\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{H^{2}}.

(by
Lemma
26
-(a))

Next, again using
Lemma
26
-(b), the last term can be upper bounded as

\displaystyle\|{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathcal{G}}}\widehat{f}-{\mathcal{T}}_{{\mathcal{G}}}f^{\star}\|_{\bar{d},h}\leq\|\widehat{f}-f^{\star}\|_{\bar{d},h+1}.

Combining all above, we get

\displaystyle\|\widehat{f}-f^{\star}\|_{\bar{d},h}\leq\|\widehat{f}-f^{\star}\|_{\bar{d},h+1}+\mathcal{O}\left(\sqrt{\bar{\mathsf{C}}^{\star}}\varepsilon_{\textup{appr}}+\sqrt{\bar{\mathsf{C}}^{\star}}\varepsilon_{\textup{stat}}+\frac{\varepsilon}{H}\right),

which gives
\|\widehat{f}-f^{\star}\|_{\bar{d},1}\leq\mathcal{O}\left(H\sqrt{\bar{\mathsf{C}}^{\star}}\varepsilon_{\textup{appr}}+H\sqrt{\bar{\mathsf{C}}^{\star}}\varepsilon_{\textup{stat}}+\varepsilon\right)
after recursively applying the inequality and using Cauchy-Schwarz inequality. The desired inequality follows by noticing that
\mathbb{E}_{{x}\sim\rho}[|\widehat{f}({x})-f^{\star}({x})|]\leq\|\widehat{f}-f^{\star}\|_{\bar{d},1}
by Cauchy-Schwarz.

\blacksquare

Proof of
Theorem
F.1
(
Theorem
5.1
in the main body).

In the
fully realizable
setting (i.e.,
V^{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}\in{\mathcal{F}}
), in order to control the magnitude of
\Phi_{\max}
, we discretize the function set
{\mathcal{F}}
, and make it an
approximate realizable
case.

For each
f\in{\mathcal{F}}
, we round the value of
f(s)
to the nearest multiple of
\frac{\varepsilon}{H\sqrt{\bar{\mathsf{C}}}}
. This way, we have
\varepsilon_{\textup{appr}}=\frac{\varepsilon}{H\sqrt{\bar{\mathsf{C}}}}
and
\Phi_{\max}=O\left(\left(\frac{H}{\varepsilon_{\textup{appr}}}\right)^{2}\right)=O\left(\frac{H^{4}\bar{\mathsf{C}}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right)
. Thus, by
Lemma
28
,

\displaystyle\|\widehat{f}-f^{\star}\|_{\bar{d},1}

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{O}\left(H\sqrt{\bar{\mathsf{C}}^{\star}}\varepsilon_{\textup{appr}}+H\sqrt{\frac{\bar{\mathsf{C}}^{\star}\Phi_{\max}\log(n|{\mathcal{F}}|\Phi_{\max}/\delta)}{n}}+\varepsilon\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{O}\left(H\sqrt{\frac{\bar{\mathsf{C}}^{2}H^{4}\log(n|{\mathcal{F}}|H\bar{\mathsf{C}}/\varepsilon\delta)}{\varepsilon^{2}n}}+\varepsilon\right).

In order to make the last expression to be
\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon)
, we need

\displaystyle n\geq\widetilde{\Omega}\left(\frac{\bar{\mathsf{C}}^{2}H^{6}\log(|{\mathcal{F}}|/\delta)}{\varepsilon^{4}}\right).

\blacksquare

F.6
Implications of the BVFT Upper Bound

Lemma 29
.

For any
\varepsilon
,
\bar{\mathsf{C}}_{\varepsilon}\leq\mathsf{C}_{\textup{pf}}
, where
\mathsf{C}_{\textup{pf}}
is defined in
Definition
B.1
.

Proof of
Lemma
29
.

By the definition of
\bar{\mathsf{C}}_{\varepsilon}
,

\displaystyle\bar{\mathsf{C}}_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\leq\max_{h}\max_{\phi\in\Phi_{h}}\frac{\bar{d}(\phi)}{\bar{\nu}(\phi)}

\displaystyle=\max_{h}\max_{\phi\in\Phi_{h}}\frac{\sum_{\phi^{\prime}\in\Phi_{h-1}}\bar{d}(\phi^{\prime})\bar{T}(\phi\mid\phi^{\prime},{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}})}{\sum_{{x}\in\phi}\mu({x},{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}({x}))}

(by the definitions of
\bar{d}
and
\bar{\nu}
and
\nu
)

\displaystyle=\max_{h}\max_{\phi\in\Phi_{h}}\frac{\sum_{\phi^{\prime}\in\Phi_{h-1}}\bar{d}(\phi^{\prime})\bar{T}(\phi\mid\phi^{\prime},{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}})}{\sum_{{x}\in\phi}\mu({x})\mu({\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}({x})\mid{x})}

(by the definition of
\mu({a}\mid{x})
)

\displaystyle\leq\max_{h}\max_{\phi\in\Phi_{h}}\frac{\sum_{\phi^{\prime}\in\Phi_{h-1}}\bar{d}(\phi^{\prime})\bar{T}(\phi\mid\phi^{\prime},{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}})}{\sum_{{x}\in\phi}\mu({x})}\cdot\mathsf{C}_{\mathcal{A}}

\displaystyle\leq\max_{h}\max_{\phi\in\Phi_{h}}\frac{1}{\sum_{{x}\in\phi}\mu({x})}\sum_{\phi^{\prime}\in\Phi_{h-1}}\bar{d}(\phi^{\prime})\frac{\sum_{{x}^{\prime}\in\phi^{\prime}}\nu({x}^{\prime})\sum_{{x}\in\phi}T({x}\mid{x}^{\prime},{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}})}{\sum_{{x}^{\prime}\in\phi^{\prime}}\nu({x}^{\prime})}\cdot\mathsf{C}_{\mathcal{A}}

(by the definition of
\bar{T}
)

\displaystyle\leq\max_{h}\max_{\phi\in\Phi_{h}}\max_{{x}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{X}}_{h-1}}\frac{\sum_{{x}\in\phi}T({x}\mid{x}^{\prime},{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}})}{\sum_{{x}\in\phi}\mu({x})}\cdot\mathsf{C}_{\mathcal{A}}

\displaystyle\leq\max_{h}\max_{{x}\in{\mathcal{X}}_{h}}\max_{{x}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{X}}_{h-1}}\frac{T({x}\mid{x}^{\prime},{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}})}{\mu({x})}\cdot\mathsf{C}_{\mathcal{A}}

\displaystyle\leq\mathsf{C}_{\mathcal{X}}\cdot\mathsf{C}_{\mathcal{A}}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{C}_{\textup{pf}}.

\blacksquare

Lemma 30
.

Let the offline data distribution
\mu
be admissible, i.e.,
\mu({x},{a})=d^{{\pi_{b}}}({x}){\pi_{b}}({a}\mid{x})
for some
{\pi_{b}}
. Suppose that
\frac{1}{\mu({\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}({x})\mid{x})}=\frac{1}{{\pi_{b}}({\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}({x})\mid{x})}\leq\mathsf{C}_{\mathcal{A}}
for all
{x}\in{\mathcal{X}}
. Then for any
\varepsilon
,
\bar{\mathsf{C}}_{\varepsilon}\leq(\mathsf{C}_{\mathcal{A}})^{H}
.

Proof of
Lemma
30
.

For a fixed
h
, we have

\displaystyle\max_{\phi\in\Phi_{h}}\frac{\bar{d}(\phi)}{\bar{\nu}(\phi)}

\displaystyle=\max_{\phi\in\Phi_{h}}\frac{\sum_{\phi^{\prime}\in\Phi_{h-1}}\bar{d}(\phi^{\prime})\bar{T}(\phi\mid\phi^{\prime},{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}})}{\sum_{{x}\in\phi}\mu({x},{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}({x}))}

(by the definitions of
\bar{d}
and
\bar{\nu}
and
\nu
)

\displaystyle=\max_{\phi\in\Phi_{h}}\frac{\sum_{\phi^{\prime}\in\Phi_{h-1}}\bar{d}(\phi^{\prime})\bar{T}(\phi\mid\phi^{\prime},{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}})}{\sum_{{x}\in\phi}\mu({x})\mu({\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}({x})\mid{x})}

(by the definition of
\mu({a}\mid{x})
)

\displaystyle=\max_{\phi\in\Phi_{h}}\frac{\sum_{\phi^{\prime}\in\Phi_{h-1}}\bar{d}(\phi^{\prime})\bar{T}(\phi\mid\phi^{\prime},{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}})}{\sum_{{x}\in\phi}\sum_{{x}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{X}}_{h-1}}\sum_{{a}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{A}}}\mu({x}^{\prime},{a}^{\prime})T({x}\mid{x}^{\prime},{a}^{\prime})\mu({\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}({x})\mid{x})}

(by the fact that
\mu
is an occupancy measure)

\displaystyle\leq\max_{\phi\in\Phi_{h}}\frac{\sum_{\phi^{\prime}\in\Phi_{h-1}}\bar{d}(\phi^{\prime})\bar{T}(\phi\mid\phi^{\prime},{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}})}{\sum_{{x}\in\phi}\sum_{{x}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{X}}_{h-1}}\mu({x}^{\prime},{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}({x}^{\prime}))T({x}\mid{x}^{\prime},{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}({x}^{\prime}))}\cdot\mathsf{C}_{\mathcal{A}}

\displaystyle=\max_{\phi\in\Phi_{h}}\frac{\sum_{\phi^{\prime}\in\Phi_{h-1}}\bar{d}(\phi^{\prime})\bar{T}(\phi\mid\phi^{\prime},{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}})}{\sum_{\phi^{\prime}\in\Phi_{h-1}}\sum_{{x}\in\phi}\sum_{{x}^{\prime}\in\phi^{\prime}}\mu({x}^{\prime},{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}({x}^{\prime}))T({x}\mid{x}^{\prime},{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}({x}^{\prime}))}\cdot\mathsf{C}_{\mathcal{A}}

\displaystyle=\max_{\phi\in\Phi_{h}}\frac{\sum_{\phi^{\prime}\in\Phi_{h-1}}\bar{d}(\phi^{\prime})\bar{T}(\phi\mid\phi^{\prime},{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}})}{\sum_{\phi^{\prime}\in\Phi_{h-1}}\left(\sum_{{x}^{\prime}\in\phi^{\prime}}\mu({x}^{\prime},{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}({x}^{\prime}))\right)\bar{T}(\phi\mid\phi^{\prime},{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}})}\cdot\mathsf{C}_{\mathcal{A}}

(by the definition of
\bar{T}
)

\displaystyle=\max_{\phi\in\Phi_{h}}\frac{\sum_{\phi^{\prime}\in\Phi_{h-1}}\bar{d}(\phi^{\prime})\bar{T}(\phi\mid\phi^{\prime},{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}})}{\sum_{\phi^{\prime}\in\Phi_{h-1}}\bar{\nu}(\phi^{\prime})\bar{T}(\phi\mid\phi^{\prime},{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}})}\cdot\mathsf{C}_{\mathcal{A}}

(by the definition of
\bar{\nu}
)

\displaystyle\leq\max_{\phi^{\prime}\in\Phi_{h-1}}\frac{\bar{d}(\phi^{\prime})}{\bar{\nu}(\phi^{\prime})}\cdot\mathsf{C}_{\mathcal{A}}.

Recursively applying this we get
\max_{h}\max_{\phi\in\Phi_{h}}\frac{\bar{d}(\phi)}{\bar{\nu}(\phi)}\leq(\mathsf{C}_{\mathcal{A}})^{H}
. Then by just noticing that
\bar{\mathsf{C}}_{\varepsilon}\leq\max_{h}\max_{\phi\in\Phi_{h}}\frac{\bar{d}(\phi)}{\bar{\nu}(\phi)}
finishes the proof.

\blacksquare

Appendix G
Role of Realizable Value Function Class in Offline RL

So far, we have considered offline policy evaluation problems where in addition to an offline data distribution
\mu
(that satisfies concentrability), the learner is also given a function class
\mathcal{F}
that contains
Q^{{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}}
—the state-action value function corresponding to the policy
{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}
that the learner wishes to evaluate. To understand the role of value function class in offline RL, in this section, we ask:

Is statistically efficient offline policy evaluation feasible without access to a realizable value function class?

We answer this question negatively for both admissible and trajectory data. Our first result in
Theorem
G.1
(below) shows that given only admissible offline data, offline policy evaluation is intractable without a realizable value function class, even when we have bounded pushforward concentrability coefficient.

Theorem G.1
.

For any positive integer
N
, there exists a class
\mathcal{M}
of
MDP
s with shared state space
\mathcal{X}_{N}
, action space
\mathcal{A}=\{\mathfrak{a}_{1},\mathfrak{a}_{2}\}
and horizon
H=3
, a deterministic evaluation policy
\pi_{e}
, and an exploration policy
{\pi_{b}}
with
\Pr\left({\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}({x})={\pi_{b}}({x})\right)\geq\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}
for all
{x}\in\mathcal{X}
such that any algorithm that estimate the value
V^{\pi_{e}}(\rho,M)
up to error
\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}
for all
MDP
s
M\in\mathcal{M}
must use
\Omega(N)
many admissible samples in some
MDP
in
\mathcal{M}
.

Theorem
G.1
suggests the intractability of offline policy evaluation without a realizable function class since the result holds for any positive integer
N
.

Remark 1
.

Note that since
H=3
in the construction of
Theorem
G.1
, the property
\Pr\left({\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}({x})={\pi_{b}}({x})\right)\geq\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}
indicates that the pushforward concentrability coefficient
\mathsf{C}_{\textup{pf}}\leq 8
w.r.t. the admissible distribution
\mu_{h}({x},{a};M)=d^{\pi_{b}}({x},{a};M)
(see
Definition
B.1
). Thus, using
Lemma
29
, the aggregated concentrability coefficient
\bar{\mathsf{C}}\leq 8
for any aggregation scheme on the underlying MDPs.

On the other hand, under access to a realizable state-action value function class (
Assumption
F.1
), BVFT algorithm (
Theorem
F.1
+
Lemma
30
) obtains a sample complexity upper bound of
O(\textup{poly}(2^{H},\log(\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert)))
which is tractable for
H=3
. This highlights the role of a realizable value function class in offline policy evaluation with admissible data. Our next result extends this to trajectory offline data.

Theorem G.2
.

There exists a class
\mathcal{M}
of
MDP
s with shared state space
\mathcal{X}
, action space
\mathcal{A}=\{\mathfrak{a}_{1},\mathfrak{a}_{2}\}
, and horizon
H
, a deterministic evaluation policy
\pi_{e}
and an exploration policy
{\pi_{b}}
such that the pushforward concentrability coefficient
\mathsf{C}_{\textup{pf}}\leq 4
for the offline distribution
\mu_{h}({x},{a};M)=d^{{\pi_{b}}}_{h}({x},{a};M)
(see
Definition
B.1
).

Furthermore, any algorithm that estimate the value
V^{\pi_{e}}(\rho,M)
up to precision
\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}
for all
MDP
s
M\in\mathcal{M}
must use
\Omega(2^{H})
many offline trajectories in some
MDP
in
\mathcal{M}
.

The above shows that agnostic offline policy evaluation is not statistically tractable even when given trajectory offline data. On the other hand, recall that under access to a realizable state-action value function class (
Assumption
F.1
) and bounded pushforward concentrability coefficient, the BVFT algorithm in
Xie and Jiang 2021
enjoys a
\textup{poly}(\mathsf{C}_{\textup{pf}},H,\log(\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert),\nicefrac{{1}}{{\varepsilon}})
sample complexity (even without access to trajectory data).

Remark 2
.

For the lower bound
MDP
construction in
Theorem
G.2
, recall that
{\pi_{b}}({x})=\mathrm{Uniform}(\mathcal{A})
for any
{x}\in\mathcal{X}
. Thus, given trajectory data, the classical importance sampling algorithm from
Kearns et al. 1999
;
Agarwal et al. 2019
can evaluate the value of
{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}
upto precision
\varepsilon
after collecting
\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{2^{H}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right)
many offline trajectories from
{\pi_{b}}
.

G.1
Proof of Lower Bounds

Figure 5:
Lower bound construction in
Theorem
G.1
. The blue arrows represent the transitions under the action
\mathfrak{a}_{1}
, and the red arrows represent the transitions under the action
\mathfrak{a}_{2}
. In the middle layer, the arrows to the blocks
\mathcal{X}_{2}^{1}
and
\mathcal{X}_{2}^{2}
denote uniform transitions to the states within those blocks.

To avoid redundancy, we only provide an informal construction of the lower bound here. A formal lower bound construction can be obtained by following arguments similar to that in
Appendix
C
.

Proof of
Theorem
G.1
.
Let
N
be a positive integer, and consider a state space
\mathcal{X}=\mathcal{X}_{1}\cup\mathcal{X}_{2}\cup\mathcal{X}_{3}
where
\mathcal{X}_{1}=\{{x}_{1}\}
,
\mathcal{X}_{3}=\{{x}_{3}\}
and
\mathcal{X}_{2}
is of size
4N^{2}
. Consider a partition
\phi
of
\mathcal{X}_{2}
that divides it into two parts
\mathcal{X}_{2}^{1}
and
\mathcal{X}_{2}^{2}
, each of size
2N^{2}
. For any such partition
\phi
, we define two MDPs
M^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}
and
M^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}
, where the
M_{\phi}^{\scriptscriptstyle(i)}=MDP(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{A},H,T^{\scriptscriptstyle(i)},r^{\scriptscriptstyle(i)},\rho)
is defined such that (this construction can be viewed in
Figure
5
):

\bullet

Horizon
H=3
, action space
\mathcal{A}=\{\mathfrak{a}_{1},\mathfrak{a}_{2}\}
, and initial distribution
\rho=\delta_{{x}_{1}}(\cdot)
.

\bullet

Transition dynamics
T^{\scriptscriptstyle(i)}
is defined such that
T^{\scriptscriptstyle(i)}(\cdot\mid{x}_{2})=\delta_{{x}_{3}}(\cdot)
for any
{x}_{2}\in\mathcal{X}_{2}
, and

\displaystyle T^{\scriptscriptstyle(i)}(\cdot\mid{x}_{1},{a})=\begin{cases}\mathrm{Uniform}(\mathcal{X}_{2}^{1})&\text{if}\quad{a}=\mathfrak{a}_{1},\\
\mathrm{Uniform}(\mathcal{X}_{2}^{2})&\text{if}\quad{a}=\mathfrak{a}_{2}.\end{cases}

\bullet

Reward function is defined for any
{a}\in\{\mathfrak{a}_{1},\mathfrak{a}_{2}\}
,

\displaystyle r^{\scriptscriptstyle(i)}({x},{a})=\begin{cases}0&\text{if}\quad{x}={x}_{1},\\
1&\text{if}\quad{x}\in\mathcal{X}^{\scriptscriptstyle(i)}_{2},\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

We thus define the class
\mathcal{M}
as

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}=\bigcup_{\phi\in\Phi}(M^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}_{\phi},M^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}_{\phi}),

where
\Phi
denotes the set of all feasible partitions for
\mathcal{X}_{2}
into
\mathcal{X}_{2}^{1}
and
\mathcal{X}_{2}^{2}
of the same size, and satisfies
\lvert\Phi\rvert=2^{O(N\log(N))}
.

We further define the evaluation policy
{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}
and
{\pi_{b}}
such that for all
{x}\in\mathcal{X}
,

\displaystyle{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}({x})=\delta_{\mathfrak{a}_{1}}(\cdot),\qquad\text{and}\qquad{\pi_{b}}({x})=\mathrm{Uniform}(\{\mathfrak{a}_{1},\mathfrak{a}_{2}\}).

Then for any
M\in\cup_{\phi\in\Phi}\mathcal{M}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}
, we have
V^{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}(\rho;M)=1
and for any
M\in\cup_{\phi\in\Phi}\mathcal{M}^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}
, we have
V^{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}(\rho;M)=0
. Hence if the algorithm cannot tell whether
M\in\cup_{\phi\in\Phi}\mathcal{M}^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}
or
M\in\cup_{\phi\in\Phi}\mathcal{M}^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}
, then the algorithm must fail to output
\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}
-accurate estimation in at least one case among
\mathcal{M}
.

The key intuition behind the proof is that given some dataset
\mathcal{D}_{h}=\{({x}_{h},{a}_{h},r_{h},{x}_{h+1})\}
, the learner can not identify which states belong to
\mathcal{X}_{2}^{1}
vs
\mathcal{X}_{2}^{2}
in the second layer. Since, the reward model depends on whether
{x}\in\mathcal{X}_{2}^{1}
or
{x}\in\mathcal{X}_{2}^{2}
, inability to identify the partition
\phi
which split
\mathcal{X}
into
\mathcal{X}_{2}^{1}
and
\mathcal{X}_{2}^{2}
, will lead to an error in evaluating
V^{{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}}(\rho;M)
with probability
\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}
since we consider
\Phi
to be the set of all possible partitions of equal size.

To see the above, note that for any
M\in\mathcal{M}
, the marginal occupancy measure at layer
2
is

d_{2}^{\pi_{b}}(\cdot;M)=\mathrm{Uniform}(\mathcal{X}_{2}\times\{\mathfrak{a}_{1},\mathfrak{a}_{2}\}).

Hence, samples
({x}_{2},{a}_{2},r_{2},{x}_{3})
where
{x}_{2}\in\mathcal{X}_{2}
cannot provide useful any information unless we know whether
{x}_{2}\in\mathcal{X}_{2}^{1}
or
\mathcal{X}_{2}^{2}
. However, while collecting admissible samples of the second layer, the distribution we samples from is
d^{\pi_{b}}_{2}(\cdot;M)
, i.e.
\mathrm{Uniform}(\mathcal{X}_{2})
, which reveals no information on
\mathcal{X}_{2}^{1}
and
\mathcal{X}_{2}^{2}
unless some state
{x}_{2}
appears both in samples
({x}_{1},a_{1},r_{1},{x}_{2})
and
({x}_{2},a_{2},r_{2})
. According to our choice of
N
, this happens with probability at most

1-\prod_{i=1}^{2N}\left(1-\frac{2N}{4N^{2}}\right)^{N}\leq\frac{1}{2}.

Hence any algorithm must fail to output
\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}
-accurate estimation of
V^{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}(\rho;M)
in at least one
M\in\mathcal{M}
with probability at least
\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}
.

\blacksquare

Figure 6:
Lower bound construction in
Theorem
G.2
. The blue arrows represent the transitions under the action
\mathfrak{a}_{1}
, and the red arrows represent the transitions under the action
\mathfrak{a}_{2}
. In layers for
h=2
to
H-1
, the arrows to the blocks
\mathcal{X}_{h}^{1}
and
\mathcal{X}_{h}^{2}

denote uniform transitions to the states within those blocks.

Proof of
Theorem
G.2
.

Let
N=2^{H}
and consider a state space
\mathcal{X}=\cup_{h=1}^{H}\mathcal{X}_{h}
where
\mathcal{X}_{1}=\{{x}_{1}\}
and
\mathcal{X}_{2},\cdots,\mathcal{X}_{H}
are of size
4N^{2}
each. Consider
\phi
to be a partition of
\mathcal{X}_{2},\mathcal{X}_{3},\cdots,\mathcal{X}_{H}
that divides each
\mathcal{X}_{h}
(
2\leq h\leq H
) into two parts
\mathcal{X}_{h}^{1}
and
\mathcal{X}_{h}^{2}
, each of size
2N^{2}
. For any such
\phi
, we define two MDPs
M^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}
and
M^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}
, where the
M_{\phi}^{\scriptscriptstyle(i)}=MDP(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{A},H,T^{\scriptscriptstyle(i)},r^{\scriptscriptstyle(i)},\rho)
is defined such that (this construction can be viewed in
Figure
6
):

\bullet

Horizon
H
, action space
\mathcal{A}=\{\mathfrak{a}_{1},\mathfrak{a}_{2}\}
, and initial distribution
\rho=\delta({x}_{1})
.

\bullet

Transition dynamics
T^{\scriptscriptstyle(i)}:\mathcal{X}_{h}\times\mathcal{A}\mapsto\mathcal{X}_{h+1}
is defined such that

\displaystyle T^{\scriptscriptstyle(i)}(\cdot\mid{x}_{1},{a})=\begin{cases}\mathrm{Uniform}(\mathcal{X}_{2}^{1})&\text{if}\quad{a}=\mathfrak{a}_{1},\\
\mathrm{Uniform}(\mathcal{X}_{2}^{1}\cup\mathcal{X}_{2}^{2})&\text{if}\quad{a}=\mathfrak{a}_{2},\end{cases}

and for
{x}\in\mathcal{X}_{h}^{1}
with
h\in[H]
,

\displaystyle T^{\scriptscriptstyle(i)}(\cdot\mid{x},{a})=\begin{cases}\mathrm{Uniform}(\mathcal{X}_{2}^{1})&\text{if}\quad{a}=\mathfrak{a}_{1},\\
\mathrm{Uniform}(\mathcal{X}_{2}^{1}\cup\mathcal{X}_{2}^{2})&\text{if}\quad{a}=\mathfrak{a}_{2},\end{cases}

for
{x}\in\mathcal{X}_{h}^{2}
with
h\in[H]
,

\displaystyle T^{\scriptscriptstyle(i)}(\cdot\mid{x},{a})=\begin{cases}\mathrm{Uniform}(\mathcal{X}_{2}^{2})&\text{if}\quad{a}=\mathfrak{a}_{1},\\
\mathrm{Uniform}(\mathcal{X}_{2}^{1}\cup\mathcal{X}_{2}^{2})&\text{if}\quad{a}=\mathfrak{a}_{2}.\end{cases}

\bullet

Reward function: for any
{a}\in\{\mathfrak{a}_{1},\mathfrak{a}_{2}\}
,

\displaystyle r^{\scriptscriptstyle(i)}({x},a)=\begin{cases}1&\text{if}\quad{x}\in\mathcal{X}^{\scriptscriptstyle(i)}_{H},\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

We thus define the class
\mathcal{M}
as

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}=\bigcup_{\phi\in\Phi}(M^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}_{\phi},M^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}_{\phi}),

where
\Phi
denotes the set of all feasible partitions for
\mathcal{X}_{2},\cdots,\mathcal{X}_{H}
into
\mathcal{X}_{h}^{1}
and
\mathcal{X}_{h}^{2}
of the same size. We further define the evaluation policy
{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}
and
{\pi_{b}}
such that for all
{x}\in\mathcal{X}
,

\displaystyle{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}({x})=\delta_{\mathfrak{a}_{1}}(\cdot),\qquad\text{and}\qquad{\pi_{b}}({x})=\mathrm{Uniform}(\{\mathfrak{a}_{1},\mathfrak{a}_{2}\}).

For any
MDP

M\in\mathcal{M}
, we observe that the occupancy measure
d_{h}^{\pi_{b}}(\cdot;M)
is

d_{h}^{\pi_{b}}(\cdot;M)=\mathrm{Uniform}(\mathcal{X}_{h}\times\mathcal{A}).

Hence it is easy to verify that the strong coverability coefficient? of any instances in
\mathcal{M}
is upper bounded by
4
. Additionally, we also have for any
MDP

M\in\bigcup_{\phi\in\Phi}M_{\phi}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}
,
V^{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}(\rho;M)=1
and for any
M\in\bigcup_{\phi\in\Phi}M_{\phi}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}
,
V^{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}(\rho;M)=0
.

The only way to tell whether a case
M\in\bigcup_{\phi\in\Phi}M_{\phi}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}
or
M\in\bigcup_{\phi\in\Phi}M_{\phi}^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}
is through the reward function in the last layer. However, if action
\mathfrak{a}_{2}
is taken in any step within the whole trajectory, the last layer distribution will be
\mathrm{Uniform}(\mathcal{X}_{H})
, which will induce the same reward distribution no matter the
MDP
is
M_{\phi}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}
or
M_{\phi}^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}
.

Hence as long as none of the trajectory collected takes only action
1
among the trajectory, the learner will fail to output
\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}
-accurate estimation in at least one
MDP
in
\mathcal{M}
with probability at least
\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}
. And using
o(2^{H})
trajectories, the learner will fail to output
\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}
-accurate estimation in at least one
MDP
in
\mathcal{M}
with probability at least
\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}-o(2^{H})\cdot\nicefrac{{1}}{{2^{H}}}\geq\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}
.

\blacksquare
